Task: Please review the file #ai-ollama-dev.qmd. I have completed all of the steps through Test Model Inference. However, the model takes forever to complete because it runs cpu only. Clearly I need to get the podman container configured to use the host computer's gpu "$ podman exec -it ollama bash

## Copilot

The most likely issue is that `ai-ollama-dev.qmd` tells you to run a plain `ollama/ollama` container, but it does not include any GPU device/runtime configuration, so Ollama is falling back to CPU inference.

I reviewed the chapter and the current container command confirms that. The file currently says to start Ollama like this:

```bash name=ai-ollama-dev.qmd url=https://github.com/MVR-GIS/MVR-User-Guide/blob/f61a36e46a934058b4a415831feb0dadddc2dff7/ai-ollama-dev.qmd#L69-L77
podman run -d \
  --name ollama \
  -p 11434:11434 \
  -v ollama:/root/.ollama \
  --restart=always \
  ollama/ollama
```

That starts the container, but it does **not** pass through the NVIDIA GPU.

Here are **4 ranked options**, highest-confidence first.

### Option 1 — Update the chapter to use a GPU-enabled Podman run command on NVIDIA hosts
**What it is:** revise `ai-ollama-dev.qmd` so the default container example includes NVIDIA GPU access for Windows/WSL2 or Linux hosts where Podman is configured for NVIDIA containers.

**Why this is my top recommendation:** it directly matches your failure mode and fixes the documentation gap at the source.

**Pros**
- Solves the real cause in the instructions.
- Best long-term maintainability.
- Helps future readers avoid CPU-only inference.

**Cons**
- Needs version-aware wording because Podman + NVIDIA setup differs by host OS.
- Should include a CPU fallback for unsupported systems.

**Tradeoffs**
- Slightly more setup/documentation complexity.
- Highest documentation value.

### Option 2 — Add a dedicated “Enable NVIDIA GPU for Ollama in Podman” subsection after “Create the Ollama Container”
**What it is:** keep the basic container instructions, then add a clearly labeled GPU acceleration section with replacement commands and verification steps.

**Pros**
- Minimizes disruption to the existing chapter.
- Easier for beginners because CPU and GPU paths are separated.
- Lets you document prerequisites explicitly.

**Cons**
- Readers may still follow the CPU-only command first.
- Slightly more duplication.

**Tradeoffs**
- Better pedagogically, but less foolproof than making GPU the primary path.

### Option 3 — Recommend running Ollama on the host OS and using Podman for other tooling only
**What it is:** avoid container GPU passthrough complexity by installing Ollama directly on the host machine, then point Continue/Aider at `http://localhost:11434`.

**Pros**
- Usually the simplest path for NVIDIA acceleration on a developer workstation.
- Fewer container/runtime edge cases.
- Easier troubleshooting.

**Cons**
- Deviates from the current “containerized Ollama” architecture.
- Less self-contained.

**Tradeoffs**
- Best operational simplicity, weaker alignment with the current chapter architecture.

### Option 4 — Keep the current container flow but add troubleshooting only
**What it is:** keep the original instructions and add a short note explaining that slow inference usually means CPU-only execution and that users should rebuild the container with GPU support.

**Pros**
- Very small doc change.
- Fastest to implement.

**Cons**
- Reactive rather than preventive.
- Leaves the main instructions misleading for GPU-capable hosts.

**Tradeoffs**
- Lowest effort, lowest quality.

## My diagnosis

Your evidence is consistent with **CPU-only container execution**:

- Host `nvidia-smi` works, so the Windows host sees the NVIDIA GPU.
- Inside the container, `ollama list` works, so Ollama itself is installed and running.
- Inside the container, `nvidia-smi` is unavailable, which strongly suggests the container was not started with NVIDIA tooling/devices exposed.
- The chapter’s `podman run` command has no GPU flags or NVIDIA runtime setup.

One nuance: `nvidia-smi` being absent in the container is not, by itself, absolute proof that GPU compute is unavailable. But combined with the current run command and your slow inference, it is the highest-confidence explanation.

## What I recommend you choose

I recommend **Option 1**, with a bit of **Option 2**:
- make the current Ollama container section explicitly CPU-only or replace it with a GPU-capable path for NVIDIA hosts,
- add a short verification subsection.

## Minimal manual fix to test now

Stop and remove the current container, then recreate it with GPU support. The exact command depends on how Podman is configured on your machine, but the general shape should be:

```bash
podman rm -f ollama
podman run -d \
  --name ollama \
  -p 11434:11434 \
  -v ollama:/root/.ollama \
  --device nvidia.com/gpu=all \
  --restart=always \
  ollama/ollama
```

If your Podman setup does **not** support `--device nvidia.com/gpu=all`, then the missing prerequisite is the NVIDIA container integration for Podman rather than Ollama itself.

## Verification steps the chapter should include

After recreating the container, verify:

1. `podman exec -it ollama ollama ps` while a model is actively serving.
2. Run a short prompt:
   `curl http://localhost:11434/api/generate -d "{\"model\":\"qwen3:8b\",\"prompt\":\"Say hello in one sentence.\",\"stream\":false}"`
3. On the host, watch `nvidia-smi` during inference.
4. Confirm GPU memory usage increases and utilization becomes non-zero.

## Important documentation gap in the current chapter

The current chapter has:
- container creation,
- API verification,
- model pull,

but it does **not** have:
- GPU prerequisites,
- GPU-aware container launch instructions,
- verification of hardware acceleration,
- CPU fallback guidance.

That gap is exactly where your workflow breaks.

## Copilot

That error means Podman **recognized the CDI-style GPU request syntax**, but it could not resolve any actual NVIDIA CDI device definitions on the system. In practice, that usually means the NVIDIA Container Toolkit/CDI spec has **not been installed or generated in the Podman VM / Linux environment** that is running the container. NVIDIA’s docs say CDI devices like `nvidia.com/gpu=all` depend on a generated CDI specification, and `nvidia-ctk cdi list` should show available devices before `podman run --device nvidia.com/gpu=all ...` can work. ([docs.nvidia.com](https://docs.nvidia.com/datacenter/cloud-native/container-toolkit/1.18.2/cdi-support.html?utm_source=openai))

So your issue is now more specific than before:

- The host Windows machine has an NVIDIA GPU.
- But the **Podman container runtime environment** does not currently have resolvable NVIDIA CDI devices.
- Therefore, the container cannot get GPU access, and Ollama stays CPU-only. ([docs.nvidia.com](https://docs.nvidia.com/datacenter/cloud-native/container-toolkit/1.18.2/cdi-support.html?utm_source=openai))

Here are the **4 best options**, ranked.

### Option 1 — Configure NVIDIA CDI in the Podman Linux environment, then rerun Ollama
**What it is:** install/configure the NVIDIA Container Toolkit where Podman actually runs, generate the CDI spec, verify with `nvidia-ctk cdi list`, then recreate the container with `--device nvidia.com/gpu=all`.

**Pros**
- Best match to the current chapter architecture.
- Uses the officially documented Podman + CDI path.
- Keeps Ollama containerized.

**Cons**
- Most setup complexity.
- On Windows + Podman Desktop, the critical environment is often the Podman machine/VM, not just Windows host setup.

**Tradeoffs**
- Best long-term doc quality, moderate-to-high troubleshooting cost.

### Option 2 — Switch the manual to recommend host-native Ollama on Windows for GPU users
**What it is:** on Windows developer workstations with NVIDIA GPUs, run Ollama directly on the host OS instead of in Podman.

**Pros**
- Simplest operational path.
- Likely much easier for your audience.
- Avoids Podman GPU passthrough complexity on Windows.

**Cons**
- Breaks the “everything in Podman” symmetry.
- Different install path by OS.

**Tradeoffs**
- Best usability, less architectural purity.

### Option 3 — Document Podman container as CPU-only by default, with GPU as advanced/experimental
**What it is:** keep the current chapter but explicitly state that the plain `podman run` example is CPU-only and add a separate advanced GPU section.

**Pros**
- Accurate with current evidence.
- Low risk.
- Easy to maintain.

**Cons**
- Does not give users a frictionless GPU path.
- Less satisfying for users with capable hardware.

**Tradeoffs**
- Safest documentation stance if you have not validated Podman+GPU on Windows end-to-end.

### Option 4 — Recommend a different container runtime for GPU use cases
**What it is:** keep Podman for general containers, but direct GPU Ollama users to a runtime/setup that has better-documented Windows GPU ergonomics.

**Pros**
- May reduce user pain.
- Honest if Podman-on-Windows is too finicky.

**Cons**
- Fragments the workflow.
- Higher cognitive load in the manual.

**Tradeoffs**
- Pragmatic, but not ideal if you want one standardized stack.

## Best next step right now

I recommend **Option 1 for diagnosis**, but **Option 2 or 3 for the manual** unless you fully validate Podman GPU on your exact Windows workflow.

## What to check next

Run these checks in the **Linux environment that backs Podman**, not just on the Windows host:

1. Check whether `nvidia-ctk` exists.
2. Check whether any CDI devices are registered.
3. Check whether a CDI spec file exists.

Typical checks are:

    podman machine ssh
    which nvidia-ctk
    nvidia-ctk cdi list
    ls /etc/cdi
    ls /var/run/cdi

If `nvidia-ctk` is missing, then the NVIDIA Container Toolkit is not installed in the Podman machine. NVIDIA documents that CDI support requires the toolkit/base package and a generated CDI specification. ([docs.nvidia.com](https://docs.nvidia.com/datacenter/cloud-native/container-toolkit/1.18.2/cdi-support.html?utm_source=openai))

If `nvidia-ctk cdi list` returns no devices, then the CDI spec has not been generated or is not visible to Podman. NVIDIA documents generating it with `nvidia-ctk cdi generate` and then using Podman’s `--device nvidia.com/gpu=all` syntax. ([docs.nvidia.com](https://docs.nvidia.com/datacenter/cloud-native/container-toolkit/1.18.2/cdi-support.html?utm_source=openai))

## Important nuance for your manual

The manual currently lists “GPU acceleration through Podman” under “Current Limitations.” That is actually a good clue: based on your test, this is not yet a validated workflow in the chapter as written. The current container instructions should therefore **not** imply GPU support.

## Recommended documentation direction

Given the repo’s review-first instructions, I suggest this wording strategy:

### Recommended doc strategy
1. Keep the current `podman run` section, but label it **CPU-only baseline**.
2. Add a new section:
   **Enable NVIDIA GPU acceleration (advanced / host-specific)**
3. State that GPU acceleration requires:
   - NVIDIA driver on host,
   - NVIDIA Container Toolkit in the Podman runtime environment,
   - resolvable CDI devices,
   - successful verification before using Ollama for heavier models.
4. Add a validation step:
   - `nvidia-ctk cdi list`
   - then a small Podman GPU smoke test
   - then Ollama inference timing check.

## Manual GPU smoke test to use before Ollama

Once CDI is configured, NVIDIA’s documented Podman example pattern is essentially:

    podman run --rm --device nvidia.com/gpu=all --security-opt=label=disable ubuntu nvidia-smi -L

That exact pattern is used in NVIDIA’s CDI documentation for Podman. ([docs.nvidia.com](https://docs.nvidia.com/datacenter/cloud-native/container-toolkit/1.19.0/cdi-support.html?utm_source=openai))

If that smoke test fails, Ollama-in-Podman GPU will also fail.

## @mpdougherty

Describe how to run these checks in the Linux environment that backs Podman. 

## Copilot

Run the checks by **SSHing into the Podman machine**, because on Windows Podman usually talks to a Linux VM rather than running Linux containers directly on the Windows host. Podman’s `podman machine ssh` command opens a shell in that backing VM. ([docs.podman.io](https://docs.podman.io/en/v4.9.0/markdown/podman-machine-ssh.1.html?utm_source=openai))

## Step 1 — Enter the Linux environment behind Podman

On your Windows host terminal, run:

    podman machine ssh

If you have multiple Podman machines and are not using the default one, use:

    podman machine ssh <machine-name>

Podman documents that this opens an interactive SSH session to the Podman-managed virtual machine. ([docs.podman.io](https://docs.podman.io/en/v4.9.0/markdown/podman-machine-ssh.1.html?utm_source=openai))

## Step 2 — Confirm you are inside the Podman VM

Once inside, run:

    uname -a
    cat /etc/os-release
    whoami

This is just a sanity check so you know you are in the Linux environment, not still on Windows. The main point is that the NVIDIA CDI checks must be run **there**, because that is where Podman resolves CDI devices for Linux containers. This follows from Podman’s machine model on Windows and NVIDIA’s CDI design. ([docs.podman.io](https://docs.podman.io/en/v4.9.0/markdown/podman-machine-ssh.1.html?utm_source=openai))

## Step 3 — Check whether `nvidia-ctk` is installed

Inside the Podman VM, run:

    which nvidia-ctk
    nvidia-ctk --version

If `which nvidia-ctk` returns nothing or `command not found`, then the NVIDIA Container Toolkit CLI is not installed in the Podman VM. NVIDIA says CDI support depends on the NVIDIA Container Toolkit or the `nvidia-container-toolkit-base` package, which provides `nvidia-ctk`. ([docs.nvidia.com](https://docs.nvidia.com/datacenter/cloud-native/container-toolkit/1.18.2/cdi-support.html?utm_source=openai))

## Step 4 — Check whether any NVIDIA CDI devices are registered

Still inside the Podman VM, run:

    nvidia-ctk cdi list

This is the key check. NVIDIA documents `nvidia-ctk cdi list` as the way to see whether CDI devices are available. If this returns no devices, Podman cannot resolve `nvidia.com/gpu=all`, which matches the error you saw. ([docs.nvidia.com](https://docs.nvidia.com/datacenter/cloud-native/container-toolkit/1.17.4/cdi-support.html?utm_source=openai))

## Step 5 — Check whether a CDI spec file exists

Inside the Podman VM, run:

    ls -l /etc/cdi
    ls -l /var/run/cdi

NVIDIA documents `/etc/cdi` and `/var/run/cdi` as typical locations for CDI specification files. If those directories are empty or missing an NVIDIA spec such as `nvidia.yaml`, that is more evidence that CDI has not been generated. ([docs.nvidia.com](https://docs.nvidia.com/datacenter/cloud-native/container-toolkit/1.13.1/cdi-support.html?utm_source=openai))

## Step 6 — If needed, generate the CDI spec

If `nvidia-ctk` exists but `nvidia-ctk cdi list` shows nothing, generate the spec inside the Podman VM:

    sudo nvidia-ctk cdi generate --output=/etc/cdi/nvidia.yaml

Then recheck:

    nvidia-ctk cdi list
    grep "  name:" /etc/cdi/nvidia.yaml

NVIDIA documents `nvidia-ctk cdi generate --output=/etc/cdi/nvidia.yaml` as the standard way to create the CDI spec and then inspect the generated device names. ([docs.nvidia.com](https://docs.nvidia.com/datacenter/cloud-native/container-toolkit/1.13.1/cdi-support.html?utm_source=openai))

## Step 7 — Run a Podman GPU smoke test from the Windows host

After exiting the VM, test whether Podman can now resolve the GPU device:

    podman run --rm --device nvidia.com/gpu=all --security-opt=label=disable ubuntu nvidia-smi -L

NVIDIA’s CDI documentation uses this Podman pattern to verify CDI-based GPU injection. If this fails, Ollama-in-Podman GPU will still fail. ([docs.nvidia.com](https://docs.nvidia.com/datacenter/cloud-native/container-toolkit/1.18.2/cdi-support.html?utm_source=openai))

## What the results mean

### Case A — `podman machine ssh` works, but `nvidia-ctk` is missing
The Podman VM does not have the NVIDIA Container Toolkit installed. CDI will not work until that is added. NVIDIA says `nvidia-ctk` comes from the toolkit or base package. ([docs.nvidia.com](https://docs.nvidia.com/datacenter/cloud-native/container-toolkit/1.18.2/cdi-support.html?utm_source=openai))

### Case B — `nvidia-ctk` exists, but `nvidia-ctk cdi list` is empty
The toolkit is present, but the CDI spec is missing or not visible. Generate `/etc/cdi/nvidia.yaml` and retry. ([docs.nvidia.com](https://docs.nvidia.com/datacenter/cloud-native/container-toolkit/1.13.1/cdi-support.html?utm_source=openai))

### Case C — CDI devices appear, but the smoke test still fails
Then the issue is deeper: Podman VM GPU passthrough, driver visibility inside the VM, or runtime configuration is still incomplete. That would be the point to decide whether this workflow should remain “advanced/experimental” in the manual. This is an inference from the documented CDI flow plus your current failure. ([docs.nvidia.com](https://docs.nvidia.com/datacenter/cloud-native/container-toolkit/1.18.2/cdi-support.html?utm_source=openai))

## Copy/paste checklist

Use this exact sequence:

    podman machine ssh

Then inside the VM:

    uname -a
    cat /etc/os-release
    which nvidia-ctk
    nvidia-ctk --version
    nvidia-ctk cdi list
    ls -l /etc/cdi
    ls -l /var/run/cdi

If needed:

    sudo nvidia-ctk cdi generate --output=/etc/cdi/nvidia.yaml
    nvidia-ctk cdi list
    grep "  name:" /etc/cdi/nvidia.yaml

Then back on the host:

    podman run --rm --device nvidia.com/gpu=all --security-opt=label=disable ubuntu nvidia-smi -L

If you paste the output of those commands, I can tell you exactly which layer is missing.

## Copilot

You’ve confirmed the immediate blocker: the **Podman VM has no NVIDIA Container Toolkit and no CDI configuration**, so `--device nvidia.com/gpu=all` cannot work in this environment.

## What your results mean

From your Podman machine shell:

- `nvidia-ctk: command not found`
- `/etc/cdi`: not found
- `/var/run/cdi`: not found

That means:
1. the NVIDIA Container Toolkit CLI is not installed in the Linux VM backing Podman,
2. no CDI spec has been generated,
3. Podman therefore has no NVIDIA devices it can resolve.

So the error:

    Error: setting up CDI devices: unresolvable CDI devices nvidia.com/gpu=all

is fully explained by the state of the Podman VM.

## One small note

`which` is also missing in that Fedora image, so use these instead next time:

    command -v nvidia-ctk
    type nvidia-ctk

## Best options from here

### Option 1 — Try to install NVIDIA toolkit inside the Podman machine
**What it is:** manually provision the Podman VM with NVIDIA Container Toolkit and CDI support.

**Pros**
- Preserves the current Podman+Ollama architecture.
- Could enable true GPU containers.

**Cons**
- Highest complexity.
- On Podman Desktop + Windows + WSL2, GPU passthrough into the Podman VM may still not be fully workable even after package install.
- Harder to support in a beginner-friendly manual.

**Recommendation**
- Good for experimentation, not yet my recommended documentation path.

### Option 2 — Change the manual to say Podman Ollama is CPU baseline, and recommend host-native Ollama for NVIDIA acceleration on Windows
**What it is:** keep the container workflow, but clearly state it is the baseline path and may run CPU-only; use native Windows Ollama when GPU acceleration is required.

**Pros**
- Most practical for your audience.
- Easy to document and verify.
- Lowest support burden.

**Cons**
- Two installation paths.
- Less “everything in a container.”

**Recommendation**
- This is my top recommendation for the manual.

### Option 3 — Keep GPU-in-Podman as advanced/experimental only
**What it is:** document the dependency on NVIDIA Container Toolkit + CDI in the Podman VM, but explicitly mark the workflow unvalidated.

**Pros**
- Accurate.
- Still gives advanced users a direction.

**Cons**
- Not a complete tested recipe.
- More caveats than guidance.

**Recommendation**
- Good as an appendix or troubleshooting note.

## My recommendation

I recommend **Option 2**, plus a small part of **Option 3**:

- label the current Podman container flow as a **CPU-compatible baseline**,
- say that **Windows NVIDIA GPU acceleration is recommended via host-native Ollama**,
- keep **GPU-in-Podman** under **advanced / experimental troubleshooting** until you validate it end-to-end.

## Why this is the best documentation choice

Your repo instructions require:
- authoritative guidance,
- no speculation,
- reproducible steps,
- clear audit trail.

Right now, “GPU through Podman” is **not reproducibly validated** in your tested environment. So the manual should not present it as a standard path.

## Suggested doc changes

Here is the documentation strategy I would use for `ai-ollama-dev.qmd`:

### Change 1 — Revise the container section title
Instead of implying this is the recommended universal path, rename it to something like:

- `## Create the Ollama Container (CPU Baseline)`
or
- `## Create the Ollama Container`

and immediately add a note:

- this container configuration is a baseline configuration,
- on some systems it will use CPU only,
- GPU acceleration requires additional host-specific setup not covered by the baseline command.

### Change 2 — Add a warning after the `podman run` command
Something like:

- The command above does not by itself enable NVIDIA GPU acceleration.
- On Windows developer workstations, the Podman virtual machine must expose NVIDIA CDI devices for GPU use.
- If this is not configured, model inference will run on CPU.

### Change 3 — Add a new section
Add:

- `## GPU Acceleration Notes`
- `### Windows Workstations`
- `### Podman GPU Support Status`
- `### Recommended Windows GPU Path`

and in that section:
- explain that host `nvidia-smi` success is not enough,
- explain that the Podman Linux VM must also have NVIDIA toolkit/CDI,
- explain that in your tested environment this was not yet configured,
- recommend host-native Ollama for Windows GPU acceleration.

### Change 4 — Update “Current Limitations”
Move from vague wording:

- `GPU acceleration through Podman`

to more precise wording like:

- optionally keep Podman for unrelated container work.

## Copilot

Pursue **Option 1** by treating this as an **experiment in the Podman VM**, not yet a documented workflow. Your latest output shows the VM is Fedora 39 on WSL2 with `dnf` available, but it currently has **no NVIDIA userspace tools, no CDI config, and no visible NVIDIA device tooling**. That means the next step is to determine whether this Podman machine can be provisioned to see a GPU at all.

Here are **4 ranked experimental paths**.

### Option 1A — Check whether the Podman VM can see any NVIDIA device nodes before installing anything
**What it is:** verify whether the WSL2-backed Podman VM exposes GPU-related device files or kernel interfaces.

**Pros**
- Lowest-risk next diagnostic step.
- Prevents wasted setup if the VM cannot see GPU hardware at all.
- Best evidence-first approach.

**Cons**
- Does not fix anything by itself.

**Tradeoffs**
- Best first move. High signal, low effort.

### Option 1B — Install PCI/device-inspection tools in the Podman VM and inspect hardware visibility
**What it is:** install lightweight diagnostics like `pciutils` and inspect whether the VM can enumerate NVIDIA-related hardware.

**Pros**
- Better observability.
- Helps distinguish “tooling missing” from “GPU not exposed.”

**Cons**
- Adds packages to the Podman VM.
- Still only diagnostic.

**Tradeoffs**
- Good second step if 1A is inconclusive.

### Option 1C — Install NVIDIA Container Toolkit in the Podman VM and attempt CDI generation
**What it is:** add the NVIDIA userspace components and try to generate `/etc/cdi/nvidia.yaml`.

**Pros**
- Direct path toward a working Podman GPU container.
- Most aligned with the eventual goal.

**Cons**
- More invasive.
- May fail if GPU devices are not exposed into the VM.
- Harder to unwind and harder to document if partly working.

**Tradeoffs**
- Best fix attempt, but only after confirming the VM can plausibly see the GPU.

### Option 1D — Abandon Podman GPU for now and switch to host-native Ollama
**What it is:** stop the experiment and use the practical path.

**Pros**
- Fastest route to a usable GPU-backed model.

**Cons**
- Does not answer whether Podman GPU can be made to work.

**Tradeoffs**
- Practical fallback, but not what you asked for first.

## My recommendation

Start with **Option 1A**, then **1B**, then **1C** only if the results look promising.

## Why

Right now we know:
- Podman VM exists and runs Linux containers.
- `dnf` is available.
- `nvidia-ctk` is missing.
- CDI dirs are missing.
- `lspci` is missing.
- We do **not** yet know whether this VM can see any GPU interface at all.

If the VM cannot see any NVIDIA-related device exposure, installing the toolkit will likely just produce more dead ends.

## Run these checks next inside `podman machine ssh`

Use this exact sequence.

    echo "=== basic gpu-related devices ==="
    ls -l /dev/dxg
    ls -l /dev/nvidia*
    
    echo "=== kernel modules ==="
    cat /proc/modules | grep -i nvidia
    cat /proc/modules | grep -i dxg
    
    echo "=== kernel messages ==="
    dmesg | grep -i nvidia | tail -n 50
    dmesg | grep -i dxg | tail -n 50
    
    echo "=== sysfs scan ==="
    find /sys -iname '*nvidia*' 2>/dev/null | head -n 50
    
    echo "=== wsl-specific hints ==="
    ls -l /usr/lib/wsl
    ls -l /usr/lib/wsl/lib

## What to look for

### Best-case signs
Any of these would be encouraging:
- `/dev/dxg` exists
- `/usr/lib/wsl/lib` exists and contains NVIDIA-related libraries
- `dxg` appears in `/proc/modules`
- `dmesg` shows GPU-related initialization

That would suggest the WSL2 environment has some GPU plumbing and may support further experimentation.

### Bad signs
If all of these are absent:
- no `/dev/dxg`
- no `/dev/nvidia*`
- no `dxg` or NVIDIA references in modules/messages
- no WSL GPU libraries

then this Podman VM likely does not have usable GPU exposure, and toolkit install will probably not be enough.

## If the checks look promising, do this next

Install a few diagnostics first:

    dnf install -y pciutils usbutils findutils

Then rerun:

    lspci | grep -i -E 'nvidia|vga|3d'
    lspci -nn
    command -v nvidia-smi
    ldconfig -p | grep -i nvidia

If the environment shows GPU-related libraries or devices, then move on to NVIDIA Container Toolkit installation.

## Likely next installation path if promising

If you get promising signals, the next experiment is:

1. Add the NVIDIA Container Toolkit repository for Fedora/RHEL-compatible packaging.
2. Install `nvidia-container-toolkit` or `nvidia-container-toolkit-base`.
3. Generate CDI:
   
       sudo nvidia-ctk cdi generate --output=/etc/cdi/nvidia.yaml

4. Verify:
   
       nvidia-ctk cdi list
       cat /etc/cdi/nvidia.yaml | head -n 80

5. Smoke test:
   
       podman run --rm --device nvidia.com/gpu=all --security-opt=label=disable ubuntu nvidia-smi -L

## Important caution

Because this Podman machine is running on **WSL2**, there are really **three layers** involved:

1. Windows host NVIDIA driver
2. WSL2 GPU exposure into the Linux environment
3. Podman + CDI + container runtime configuration

You have only confirmed layer 1 so far. We are now testing layers 2 and 3.

## My current confidence

**High confidence** that the next best step is to test whether the Podman VM has any GPU visibility at all before attempting toolkit installation.

Paste the output of the exact block above, and I’ll tell you whether it’s worth proceeding to toolkit install or whether the VM lacks the necessary GPU exposure.

## Copilot

This is promising: the Podman VM **does have WSL2 GPU plumbing**.

## What your output tells us

You have all of these positive signals:

- `/dev/dxg` exists
- `dmesg` shows `dxgkrnl`
- `/usr/lib/wsl/lib` exists
- `/usr/lib/wsl/lib/nvidia-smi` exists
- WSL NVIDIA libraries like `libcuda.so`, `libnvidia-ml.so.1`, and encoder libs are present

That means **layer 2 is present**: the Linux environment backing Podman appears to have WSL2-mediated access to the Windows GPU stack.

What is still missing is **layer 3**:

- `nvidia-ctk` is not installed
- no CDI directories/specs exist
- Podman cannot resolve `nvidia.com/gpu=all`

So now it is reasonable to continue with **Option 1C**.

## Ranked next steps

### Option 1C.1 — First verify whether WSL-side `nvidia-smi` actually runs
**What it is:** run the WSL-shipped `nvidia-smi` binary directly inside the Podman VM.

**Pros**
- Fastest confirmation that GPU visibility is functional, not just libraries-on-disk.
- Lowest risk.

**Cons**
- Still diagnostic only.

**Tradeoff**
- Best immediate next move.

### Option 1C.2 — Install NVIDIA Container Toolkit base/tooling in the Podman VM
**What it is:** add `nvidia-ctk`, then generate CDI config.

**Pros**
- Directly addresses the current blocker.
- Most likely path to working `--device nvidia.com/gpu=all`.

**Cons**
- May require repo setup/package compatibility work on Fedora 39.
- Still may fail depending on Podman/WSL edge cases.

### Option 1C.3 — Try a manual CDI spec only after toolkit install
**What it is:** use `nvidia-ctk cdi generate` to produce `/etc/cdi/nvidia.yaml`.

**Pros**
- Official Podman/CDI path.
- Cleanest runtime integration if it works.

**Cons**
- Depends on successful toolkit install first.

## Recommended next action

Do these **two checks first** inside `podman machine ssh`:

    /usr/lib/wsl/lib/nvidia-smi
    LD_LIBRARY_PATH=/usr/lib/wsl/lib /usr/lib/wsl/lib/nvidia-smi

If either of those works and shows the GPU, then proceed to toolkit installation.

## Why this matters

You already have the WSL NVIDIA userspace files, but I want to confirm they are actually executable and linked correctly in this Fedora 39 Podman machine. If `nvidia-smi` runs here, then the environment is much more likely to support CDI generation successfully.

## If `nvidia-smi` works, next try these package searches

Still inside the Podman VM:

    dnf search nvidia-container-toolkit
    dnf search nvidia-container-toolkit-base
    dnf search nvidia-ctk

If Fedora repos already contain suitable packages, great. If not, we will need to add NVIDIA’s package repository.

## Likely installation path if package search fails

If the package search returns nothing useful, the next experimental route is:

1. Add NVIDIA’s libnvidia-container repository
2. Install toolkit/base package
3. Generate CDI spec
4. Retry container smoke test

But I do **not** want to skip directly to repo setup until we confirm `nvidia-smi` works inside the VM.

## Success criteria for the experiment

We are aiming for this chain:

1. `nvidia-smi` runs inside Podman VM
2. `nvidia-ctk` installs successfully
3. `nvidia-ctk cdi generate --output=/etc/cdi/nvidia.yaml`
4. `nvidia-ctk cdi list` shows devices
5. `podman run --rm --device nvidia.com/gpu=all ...` works
6. Ollama container can be recreated with CDI GPU device

## One note on the `dxg` messages

The `dxgkio_*` errors in `dmesg` are not ideal, but they do **not** by themselves prove failure. The stronger evidence is that the WSL GPU libraries and `nvidia-smi` binary are present. We need the actual `nvidia-smi` run result before concluding more.

## Ranked next options

### Option 1C.2a — Install the Fedora NVIDIA container toolkit package first
**What it is:** use the Fedora package that appeared in `dnf search`, then check whether it installs `nvidia-ctk`.

**What it is:** switch to NVIDIA’s maintained packaging if Fedora’s package does not give you `nvidia-ctk`.

**Pros**
- More likely to match NVIDIA’s CDI docs exactly
- Better long-term compatibility with the documented workflow

**Cons**

- Slightly higher maintenance burden

### Option 1C.3 — Generate CDI and run a smoke test
**What it is:** once `nvidia-ctk` exists, generate `/etc/cdi/nvidia.yaml`, list devices, and run a GPU container test.

**Pros**
- Direct proof whether Podman GPU containers work
- Gives you a clean yes/no outcome

**Cons**
- Depends on successful toolkit install first

Inside `podman machine ssh`, run:

    dnf info golang-github-nvidia-container-toolkit
    dnf install -y golang-github-nvidia-container-toolkit
    command -v nvidia-ctk
    find / -name nvidia-ctk 2>/dev/null | head -n 20

## What we’re testing

We need to know whether that Fedora package actually provides the operational CLI binary `nvidia-ctk`.

Possible outcomes:

### Best case
`command -v nvidia-ctk` returns a path.

Then immediately continue with:

    mkdir -p /etc/cdi
    nvidia-ctk cdi generate --output=/etc/cdi/nvidia.yaml
    nvidia-ctk cdi list
    grep "  name:" /etc/cdi/nvidia.yaml

If that works, exit the VM and smoke test from Windows host:

    podman run --rm --device nvidia.com/gpu=all --security-opt=label=disable ubuntu /usr/bin/env bash -lc '/usr/lib/wsl/lib/nvidia-smi'

### Middling case
The package installs but does **not** provide `nvidia-ctk`.

Then Fedora’s package is not enough, and we move to NVIDIA’s official repo.

### Worst case
The package cannot be installed or is clearly just source/devel material.

Then skip straight to NVIDIA’s official repo.

## Important practical note

Because `nvidia-smi` lives at `/usr/lib/wsl/lib/nvidia-smi` in this environment, the eventual smoke test may need to call that explicit path rather than plain `nvidia-smi`, unless you set PATH inside the test container environment.

## Why this is now a real possibility

Earlier, this looked uncertain. Now it looks technically plausible because:
- WSL GPU bridge is present,
- the Podman machine can run `nvidia-smi`,
- Fedora repos at least know about an NVIDIA container toolkit package.

The missing piece is no longer “can the VM see the GPU?”; it is now specifically “can we install the CDI/toolkit plumbing for Podman?”

## @mpdougherty

The ps prompt never returned control. It appears like it hung. II closed the ps terminal and opened a new one, but it is also not responding. What do I do now?

## Copilot

Force-close the stuck PowerShell process and start a fresh terminal, then verify whether the `podman machine ssh` session is still alive or whether the whole Podman CLI is blocked.

Here are the **best options**, ranked.

### Option 1 — Kill the stuck PowerShell window and start clean
**What it is:** terminate the hanging shell process from another terminal or Task Manager.

**Pros**
- Fastest recovery
- Lowest risk
- Usually enough if only the interactive shell is hung

**Cons**
- You lose that shell session

### Option 2 — Check whether the Podman machine itself is still healthy
**What it is:** from a fresh terminal, test `podman machine list` and `podman info`.

**Pros**
- Tells us whether only the shell hung or Podman is unhealthy
- Good next diagnostic step

**Cons**
- May also hang if the backend is wedged

### Option 3 — Restart the Podman machine
**What it is:** stop/start or restart the VM.

**Pros**
- Clears many transient hangs
- Good if the remote session wedged the backend

**Cons**
- Interrupts containers and active sessions

### Option 4 — Reboot Podman Desktop / Windows session
**What it is:** full reset if the Podman machine commands stop responding entirely.

**Pros**
- Strongest reset

**Cons**
- Most disruptive

## Recommended recovery sequence

Do this in order.

## Step 1 — Kill the hung shell
Open **Task Manager** and end the stuck `powershell.exe` or `pwsh.exe` process.

Or from **Command Prompt** or a new PowerShell window, run one of these:

    tasklist | findstr /i "powershell pwsh"
    taskkill /F /IM powershell.exe
    taskkill /F /IM pwsh.exe

If you had only one PowerShell open, this is the quickest fix.

## Step 2 — Open a fresh terminal and test whether Podman responds
Run:

    podman machine list
    podman ps -a
    podman info

### Interpret the results
- If these return normally, the hang was probably limited to the interactive SSH session.
- If they also hang, the Podman backend or machine is likely wedged.

## Step 3 — If Podman is wedged, restart the machine
Run:

    podman machine stop
    podman machine start

Then retest:

    podman machine list
    podman info
    podman ps -a

If `stop` hangs, try:

    podman machine stop --force

if your installed Podman version supports it. If not, use Podman Desktop or restart the Podman Desktop app.

## Step 4 — If needed, restart Podman Desktop
If CLI commands still do not respond:

1. Quit Podman Desktop completely
2. Reopen Podman Desktop
3. Wait for the machine to show healthy
4. Retest:

    podman machine list
    podman info

## Step 5 — If still bad, reboot Windows
This is the last resort, but it will clear a stuck WSL2/Podman state.

## Important note about the likely cause
What probably happened is that the previous command sequence or SSH session got stuck in the VM or transport layer, not necessarily that the system is damaged. Since you had been working inside `podman machine ssh`, an interactive session hang can leave the local PowerShell window waiting indefinitely.

## After recovery, use a safer pattern
Once things are responsive again, avoid long interactive SSH sessions for the next steps. Instead, run one-shot commands from Windows like this:

    podman machine ssh "command -v nvidia-ctk"
    podman machine ssh "mkdir -p /etc/cdi && nvidia-ctk cdi generate --output=/etc/cdi/nvidia.yaml"
    podman machine ssh "nvidia-ctk cdi list"
    podman machine ssh "sed -n '1,220p' /etc/cdi/nvidia.yaml"

That reduces the risk of getting trapped in a hanging shell.

## My recommendation
Use this exact sequence now:

    taskkill /F /IM powershell.exe
    taskkill /F /IM pwsh.exe

Then open a new terminal and run:

    podman machine list
    podman info
    podman ps -a

If those work, continue with **one-shot `podman machine ssh "..."` commands**, not an interactive session.

If you want, after you recover the terminal, I can give you the **exact next one-shot commands** to generate the CDI file safely.

## Copilot

Great — **you have working CDI discovery now**. The key result is:

- `nvidia-ctk cdi list` returned `nvidia.com/gpu=all`

That means the original failure is fixed. Podman can now resolve the GPU CDI device name. The generated CDI spec also shows exactly what we hoped for:

- WSL mode was auto-detected
- `/dev/dxg` is mapped
- NVIDIA libraries are mounted
- `nvidia-smi` is symlinked into `/usr/bin/nvidia-smi`

## Where we are now

You have successfully cleared the missing integration layer:

1. Windows host driver works
2. Podman VM sees GPU through WSL2
3. `nvidia-ctk` is installed
4. CDI spec exists
5. `nvidia.com/gpu=all` resolves

So the next step is no longer diagnosis. It is **runtime verification with an actual container**.

## Ranked next options

### Option 1D — Run a minimal GPU smoke test container first
**What it is:** start a tiny test container with `--device nvidia.com/gpu=all` and verify `nvidia-smi` works inside it.

**Pros**
- Lowest-risk validation
- Isolates Podman GPU runtime from Ollama
- Best evidence before rebuilding Ollama

**Cons**
- One extra step

**Tradeoff**
- Best next step

### Option 1E — Recreate the Ollama container with GPU immediately
**What it is:** remove the existing `ollama` container and start it with CDI enabled.

**Pros**
- Fast path to your real goal

**Cons**
- If it fails, you won’t know whether the problem is Podman GPU runtime or something Ollama-specific

**Tradeoff**
- Faster, but weaker troubleshooting sequence

## My recommendation

Per your repo’s “inspect first / evidence first” rules: choose **Option 1D first**, then **1E**.

## Step 1 — Run the Podman GPU smoke test

From Windows PowerShell, run:

    podman run --rm --device nvidia.com/gpu=all --security-opt=label=disable ubuntu bash -lc "nvidia-smi"

### What success looks like
You should see NVIDIA-SMI output from inside the container.

### If `nvidia-smi` is not found
That would be slightly surprising, because the CDI spec includes a hook to create `/usr/bin/nvidia-smi`. But if it happens, run:

    podman run --rm --device nvidia.com/gpu=all --security-opt=label=disable ubuntu bash -lc "ls -l /usr/bin/nvidia-smi; ls -l /dev/dxg"

That will tell us whether the hook ran and whether the device node is present.

## Step 2 — If smoke test works, recreate Ollama with GPU

Then run:

    podman rm -f ollama

    podman run -d `
      --name ollama `
      -p 11434:11434 `
      -v ollama:/root/.ollama `
      --device nvidia.com/gpu=all `
      --security-opt=label=disable `
      --restart=always `
      ollama/ollama

PowerShell note: I used backticks above because you are on Windows PowerShell.

## Step 3 — Verify Ollama container sees the GPU

Run:

    podman exec -it ollama bash -lc "nvidia-smi"

If that works, then verify Ollama inference:

    curl http://localhost:11434/api/generate -d '{
      "model":"qwen3:8b",
      "prompt":"Write one sentence about GPU acceleration.",
      "stream":false
    }'

And while that request is running, watch GPU usage on the host:

    nvidia-smi

## Expected outcomes

### Best case
- Smoke test works
- `podman exec ... nvidia-smi` works in Ollama container
- GPU memory/utilization increases during inference
- problem solved

### Middle case
- Smoke test works
- Ollama container starts, but `nvidia-smi` or inference still doesn’t use GPU

Then the problem is likely Ollama-specific runtime behavior rather than Podman CDI.

### Failure case
- Smoke test fails

Then Podman CDI generation succeeded, but runtime injection is still incomplete. That is fixable, but it’s a different layer.

## Documentation implication

You have now moved “GPU acceleration through Podman” from vague limitation to an **experimentally validated setup path in a WSL2-backed Podman machine**, pending the final Ollama smoke test.

## My confidence

**High confidence** that you should run the generic smoke test first, and that you now have a realistic chance of getting Ollama GPU acceleration working in Podman.

Paste the output of this next command:

    podman run --rm --device nvidia.com/gpu=all --security-opt=label=disable ubuntu bash -lc "nvidia-smi"

and I’ll tell you whether to proceed directly to recreating `ollama`.

## Copilot

This is just a **PowerShell syntax issue**, not a Podman/Ollama GPU failure.

In Windows PowerShell, `curl` is usually an alias for `Invoke-WebRequest`, so the Unix-style `curl -d '...'` command does not behave the way it does in Bash.

## Best options

### Option 1 — Use `curl.exe` explicitly
**What it is:** call the real curl binary instead of the PowerShell alias.

**Pros**
- Closest to the command already in the chapter
- Easy to translate from Linux examples

**Cons**
- Still a little awkward with JSON quoting in PowerShell

### Option 2 — Use PowerShell-native `Invoke-RestMethod`
**What it is:** send JSON the PowerShell way.

**Pros**
- Most reliable in PowerShell
- Best for Windows documentation
- Cleaner quoting

**Cons**
- Different from Bash examples

### Option 3 — Run the curl command from Git Bash / WSL
**What it is:** use a shell that supports the original command as written.

**Pros**
- No quoting translation needed

**Cons**
- Adds shell-switching complexity

## My recommendation

Use **Option 2** in PowerShell.

## PowerShell-native Ollama test

Run this exactly:

    $body = @{
      model  = "qwen3:8b"
      prompt = "Write one sentence about GPU acceleration."
      stream = $false
    } | ConvertTo-Json

    Invoke-RestMethod `
      -Uri "http://localhost:11434/api/generate" `
      -Method Post `
      -ContentType "application/json" `
      -Body $body

## If you want to use real curl instead

Run:

    curl.exe http://localhost:11434/api/generate `
      -H "Content-Type: application/json" `
      -d "{\"model\":\"qwen3:8b\",\"prompt\":\"Write one sentence about GPU acceleration.\",\"stream\":false}"

Your message says “All steps run as expected until:” which suggests the earlier steps, including the container recreation and likely the smoke test, succeeded.

So at this point:
- CDI generation worked

- this latest error is only the PowerShell `curl` alias issue

In a second terminal:

### 3. Also check from inside the Ollama container
Run:

If both API inference works and `nvidia-smi` inside the container works, then you’ve effectively validated GPU-enabled Ollama in Podman.

This is a documentation issue in `ai-ollama-dev.qmd` too: the chapter currently uses Bash-style `curl` examples, but your environment is Windows PowerShell. The manual should either:

- explicitly say to run these commands in Bash/WSL/Git Bash.

and the `Invoke-RestMethod` call, and I’ll tell you whether the GPU path is fully working.

## Copilot

Good — the inference request succeeded. That means **Ollama is responding correctly** from your Git Bash shell.

But this output alone does **not yet prove GPU inference**. It proves:
- the API is up,
- the model runs,
- the command syntax is now correct in Git Bash.

We still need to verify whether the model is using the GPU rather than CPU.

## Best next options

### Option 1 — Check `nvidia-smi` while inference is running
**What it is:** run a longer inference and watch host GPU memory/utilization in parallel.

**Pros**
- Fastest real-world confirmation
- Most relevant to your original problem

**Cons**
- Short prompts may finish too quickly to observe

### Option 2 — Check inside the Ollama container with `nvidia-smi`
**What it is:** verify the container can see NVIDIA tooling/devices.

**Pros**
- Strong infrastructure check
- Tells us whether CDI injection made it into the real container

**Cons**
- Still indirect; container visibility alone does not prove Ollama is actually using GPU

### Option 3 — Inspect Ollama logs/runtime behavior
**What it is:** check container logs for GPU detection clues.

**Pros**
- Useful supporting evidence

**Cons**
- Less definitive than `nvidia-smi` during active inference

## My recommendation

Do **Option 2 and Option 1**, in that order.

## Step 1 — Verify the Ollama container itself sees the GPU

From PowerShell or Git Bash, run:

    podman exec -it ollama bash -lc "nvidia-smi"

### Success means
- the container has GPU tooling available
- the CDI setup is being applied to the actual Ollama container

If that fails, the generic smoke test may work while the existing Ollama container was created before the CDI path was ready.

## Step 2 — Run a longer prompt and watch GPU usage

Open one terminal and run:

    watch -n 1 nvidia-smi

If `watch` is unavailable on Windows, just rerun `nvidia-smi` repeatedly in another shell, or use the host PowerShell window.

Then in Git Bash run a longer request, for example:

    curl http://localhost:11434/api/generate -d '{
      "model":"qwen3:8b",
      "prompt":"Write a detailed 12-paragraph explanation of how GPU acceleration works for local large language model inference, including memory transfer, matrix multiplication, quantization, batching, and tradeoffs.",
      "stream":false
    }'

### What to look for
During inference, on the host `nvidia-smi` output you want to see:
- GPU memory usage increase above 0 MiB
- GPU utilization become non-zero
- possibly the Ollama process listed

That is the strongest practical confirmation.

## Step 3 — Check Ollama logs for supporting evidence

Run:

    podman logs ollama --tail 200

Look for references to:
- CUDA
- GPU
- inference runner
- detected devices

This is supporting evidence, not the primary test.

## Important observation from your successful response

Your output includes:
- `total_duration`
- `load_duration`
- `eval_duration`

Those timings confirm the model is working, but they do not by themselves identify CPU vs GPU. A fast or slow result is not enough proof without hardware telemetry.

## Likely outcome

Given that:
- WSL GPU works
- CDI is configured
- Podman can resolve `nvidia.com/gpu=all`

there is now a **good chance** the Ollama container can use the GPU. But I would not call it confirmed until either:
1. `podman exec ... nvidia-smi` works, and
2. host `nvidia-smi` shows usage during inference.

It’s working — **the Ollama container is using the GPU**.

## What failed originally

The root cause was:

- `ai-ollama-dev.qmd` used a plain Podman container command with no GPU configuration
- the Podman VM also lacked NVIDIA Container Toolkit / CDI setup
- therefore `nvidia.com/gpu=all` was initially unresolvable
- after installing `nvidia-ctk` and generating CDI config in the Podman machine, the Ollama container could access `/dev/dxg` and WSL NVIDIA libraries

Part 1 of 2 — replace the current Ollama setup section from `## Prerequisites` through `## Test Model Inference`

    ## Prerequisites
    
    Before continuing, complete the following chapters:
    
    * Positron Getting Started
    * Podman Desktop
    * Podman Desktop Getting Started
    
    You should also verify that:
    
    * Podman Desktop is installed and functioning.
    * Podman can run Linux containers.
    * Positron is installed.
    * Git is installed and configured.
    
    This workflow assumes the following platform for GPU-accelerated local inference:
    
    * Windows host workstation
    * NVIDIA GPU with current Windows NVIDIA drivers
    * Podman machine running on WSL2
    * Git Bash or another Bash-compatible shell for Bash examples
    
    If the workstation does not provide an NVIDIA GPU, or if Podman GPU integration is not configured, Ollama may run in CPU-only mode and model responses may be significantly slower.
    
    ## Architecture
    
    The development environment consists of the following components:
    
        Positron
        ├── Continue
        └── Local source code repositories
        
        Terminal
        └── Aider
        
        Podman
        └── Ollama
        
        Ollama
        └── Local LLM Models
    
    ### Component Roles
    
    #### Positron
    
    [Positron](https://positron.posit.co/welcome.html) serves as the primary Integrated Development Environment (IDE) for:
    
    * R development
    * Python development
    * Quarto documentation
    * Git repo maintanence operations
    
    This approach depends on VS Code [Extensions for Positron](https://positron.posit.co/extensions.html) described in the next sections.
    
    #### Continue
    
    [Continue - open-source AI code agent](https://open-vsx.org/extension/Continue/continue) provides:
    
    * Code explanation
    * Code generation
    * Documentation assistance
    * Architectural discussions
    * Interactive development support
    
    Continue operates directly in a chat window inside Positron.
    
    #### Aider Composer
    
    [Aider Composer](https://open-vsx.org/extension/lee2py/aider-composer) provides:
    
    * Multi-file refactoring
    * Repository-wide code modifications
    * Git-aware code changes
    * Feature implementation assistance
    
    Aider is executed from a terminal within the repository.
    
    #### Ollama
    
    [Ollama](https://ollama.com/) provides local LLM inference. Both Continue and Aider communicate with Ollama through a local HTTP API.
    
    ## Verify Host GPU Availability
    
    Before configuring the container, verify that the Windows host can see the NVIDIA GPU.
    
    In PowerShell, run:
    
        nvidia-smi
    
    A successful result should show:
    
    * the installed NVIDIA GPU,
    * driver version information,
    * available GPU memory.
    
    If this command fails, resolve the host GPU driver installation before continuing.
    
    ## Verify the Podman Machine
    
    This workflow depends on the Podman machine running as a Linux environment on WSL2.
    
    In PowerShell, run:
    
        podman machine list
        podman info
    
    Confirm that the Podman machine is running before continuing.
    
    ## Install NVIDIA Container Toolkit in the Podman Machine
    
    GPU access inside Podman containers requires NVIDIA container tooling inside the Linux environment that backs Podman.
    
    In PowerShell, run:
    
        podman machine ssh "dnf install -y golang-github-nvidia-container-toolkit"
    
    Verify that `nvidia-ctk` is available:
    
        podman machine ssh "command -v nvidia-ctk"
    
    Expected output should include:
    
        /usr/bin/nvidia-ctk
    
    ## Generate the NVIDIA CDI Configuration
    
    Podman uses Container Device Interface (CDI) device definitions to expose the host GPU to Linux containers.
    
    Generate the CDI configuration:
    
        podman machine ssh "mkdir -p /etc/cdi && nvidia-ctk cdi generate --output=/etc/cdi/nvidia.yaml"
    
    Verify that Podman can resolve the GPU CDI device:
    
        podman machine ssh "nvidia-ctk cdi list"
    
    Expected output should include:
    
        nvidia.com/gpu=all
    
    If `nvidia.com/gpu=all` is not present, do not continue. Resolve CDI configuration before creating the Ollama container.
    
    ## Create the Ollama Container
    
    Remove any previous Ollama container before creating the GPU-enabled container.
    
    In PowerShell, run:
    
        podman rm -f ollama
    
    In Git Bash, run:
    
        podman run -d \
          --name ollama \
          -p 11434:11434 \
          -v ollama:/root/.ollama \
          --device nvidia.com/gpu=all \
          --security-opt=label=disable \
          --restart=always \
          ollama/ollama
    
    In PowerShell, use:
    
        podman run -d `
          --name ollama `
          -p 11434:11434 `
          -v ollama:/root/.ollama `
          --device nvidia.com/gpu=all `
          --security-opt=label=disable `
          --restart=always `
          ollama/ollama
    
    Verify that the container is running:
    
        podman ps
    
    Example output:
    
        CONTAINER ID  IMAGE                  STATUS
        xxxxxxxxxxxx  ollama/ollama:latest   Up
    
    ## Verify GPU Access Inside the Container
    
    Confirm that the running Ollama container can access the GPU.
    
    In Git Bash or PowerShell, run:
    
        podman exec -it ollama bash -lc "nvidia-smi"
    
    A successful result should show:
    
    * the NVIDIA GPU,
    * nonzero or available GPU memory,
    * and, during active inference, the Ollama inference process.
    
    If `nvidia-smi` does not work inside the container, do not continue to model setup. Recheck the Podman machine CDI configuration.
    
    ## Verify the Ollama API
    
    Test that the Ollama service is available.
    
    In Git Bash, run:
    
        curl http://localhost:11434/api/tags
    
    In PowerShell, run:
    
        Invoke-RestMethod -Uri "http://localhost:11434/api/tags" -Method Get
    
    A JSON response indicates that the service is functioning correctly.
    
    ## Download a Model
    
    Enter the container:
    
        podman exec -it ollama bash
    
    Download a model:
    
        ollama pull qwen3:8b
    
    Model downloads may take several hours depending on network conditions.
    
    List installed models:
    
        ollama list
    
    Example output:
    
        NAME       SIZE
        qwen3:8b   ...
    
    ## Test Model Inference
    
    Verify that the model can generate responses.
    
    In Git Bash, run:
    
        curl http://localhost:11434/api/generate -d '{
          "model":"qwen3:8b",
          "prompt":"Write an R function that buffers sf geometries.",
          "stream":false
        }'
    
    In PowerShell, run:
    
        $body = @{
          model  = "qwen3:8b"
          prompt = "Write an R function that buffers sf geometries."
          stream = $false
        } | ConvertTo-Json
        
        Invoke-RestMethod `
          -Uri "http://localhost:11434/api/generate" `
          -Method Post `
          -ContentType "application/json" `
          -Body $body
    
    A successful response confirms that local inference is functioning.
    
    To confirm that inference is using the GPU, monitor GPU activity on the host while a prompt is running:
    
        nvidia-smi
    
    During active inference, GPU memory usage should increase and the Ollama inference process should be visible.

Part 2 of 2 — replace the current troubleshooting and limitations sections

    ## Troubleshooting
    
    ### Ollama API Not Available
    
    Verify that the container is running:
    
        podman ps
    
    Verify that the API is reachable.
    
    In Git Bash:
    
        curl http://localhost:11434/api/tags
    
    In PowerShell:
    
        Invoke-RestMethod -Uri "http://localhost:11434/api/tags" -Method Get
    
    Restart the container if necessary:
    
        podman restart ollama
    
    ### GPU Device Not Available in Podman
    
    Verify that the host GPU is available:
    
        nvidia-smi
    
    Verify that the Podman machine can resolve the CDI device:
    
        podman machine ssh "nvidia-ctk cdi list"
    
    Expected output should include:
    
        nvidia.com/gpu=all
    
    If the CDI device is missing, regenerate the CDI configuration:
    
        podman machine ssh "mkdir -p /etc/cdi && nvidia-ctk cdi generate --output=/etc/cdi/nvidia.yaml"
    
    Verify GPU visibility inside the container:
    
        podman exec -it ollama bash -lc "nvidia-smi"
    
    If the container cannot run `nvidia-smi`, recreate the Ollama container with:
    
    * `--device nvidia.com/gpu=all`
    * `--security-opt=label=disable`
    
    ### Model Not Found
    
    List installed models:
    
        podman exec -it ollama ollama list
    
    Download the required model if it is not present.
    
    ### Continue Cannot Connect
    
    Verify API compatibility:
    
    In Git Bash:
    
        curl http://localhost:11434/v1/models
    
    In PowerShell:
    
        Invoke-RestMethod -Uri "http://localhost:11434/v1/models" -Method Get
    
    Review Continue configuration settings.
    
    ### Aider Cannot Connect
    
    Verify API compatibility:
    
    In Git Bash:
    
        curl http://localhost:11434/v1/models
    
    In PowerShell:
    
        Invoke-RestMethod -Uri "http://localhost:11434/v1/models" -Method Get
    
    Review Aider configuration settings.
    
    ### Bash and PowerShell Command Differences
    
    Several commands in this chapter use Bash syntax.
    
    On Windows workstations:
    
    * Use Git Bash for Bash-style `curl` examples and multi-line commands with `\`.
    * Use PowerShell-specific examples where provided.
    * Do not assume that `curl` in PowerShell behaves the same as `curl` in Bash.
    
    ## Current Limitations
    
    This workflow is currently under evaluation.
    
    Known areas requiring additional testing include:
    
    * Continue integration details
    * Aider integration details
    * Repository-scale refactoring performance
    * Validation on additional Windows workstation configurations
    * Validation on non-Windows host operating systems
    
    GPU acceleration with Podman has been validated for a Windows workstation using:
    
    * WSL2-backed Podman machine
    * NVIDIA GPU
    * NVIDIA container toolkit installed in the Podman machine
    * CDI device configuration generated with `nvidia-ctk`

These changes are all visible in the commit diff for `ai-ollama-dev.qmd`.  

## Overall assessment

**Direct answer:** this is a strong and largely correct documentation update, and it captures the validated workflow you tested. It is a meaningful improvement over the prior CPU-only instructions.

That said, I see **3 high-value fixes** I would make before considering the chapter fully polished.

## Highest-priority issues

### 1. Duplicate sentence in “Test Model Inference”
In the updated chapter, the paragraph after the GPU verification note now effectively says:

- “During active inference, GPU memory usage should increase and the Ollama inference process should be visible.”
- followed by another “A successful response confirms that local inference is functioning.”

This duplicated confirmation text appears in the source diff and also propagated into the rendered HTML/search output. It is minor, but it reads like an editing artifact rather than intentional prose.

**Recommendation:** remove the second repeated sentence.

### 2. The toolkit package name is very Fedora-39-specific
The chapter currently instructs users to install:

- `golang-github-nvidia-container-toolkit`

That is accurate for **your current Podman machine image**, and the commit correctly reflects what you actually validated. But it is tightly coupled to the Fedora 39 Podman machine packaging state rather than a more general “NVIDIA Container Toolkit” concept. This matters because Podman machine images or Fedora package naming could change over time.

**Recommendation:** either:
- keep the exact package, but explicitly label it as the **current Fedora-based Podman machine package**, or
- phrase it as “install the NVIDIA container toolkit package available in the Podman machine” and keep the exact package in a note/example.

Given your repo instructions on reproducibility and no speculation, I would not replace it with a more generic command unless you have tested that alternative. But I **would** add one sentence clarifying that this package name is validated for the current Fedora Podman machine environment.

### 3. The “Current Limitations” section no longer mentions large-model performance
Before the commit, “Large-model performance” was listed as a known area requiring additional testing. The new version removes it. That may be intentional, but based on the evidence in this chat, you validated **GPU enablement**, not broad large-model performance characteristics across model sizes and quantizations.

**Recommendation:** consider restoring “Large-model performance” unless you deliberately want to scope this chapter only to the validated `qwen3:8b` class of workflow.

## Medium-priority observations

### 4. Good change: the chapter now matches observed shell behavior
Adding Git Bash vs PowerShell variants is absolutely the right correction. Your PowerShell `curl` failure demonstrated that the prior docs would mislead Windows users. The new split instructions directly address that.

### 5. Good change: the chapter now includes actual GPU verification
Adding:

- `podman machine ssh "nvidia-ctk cdi list"`
- `podman exec -it ollama bash -lc "nvidia-smi"`

is excellent. These are the most important validation steps in the workflow and significantly improve auditability.

### 6. Good change: rendered docs were updated consistently
The commit also updates the generated HTML and search index, which is exactly what I would expect in a Quarto-based user manual workflow. That keeps source and published artifacts aligned.

- Reflects a real validated workflow
- Good enough for practical use

**Cons**
- Leaves a duplicated sentence
- Leaves package naming context implicit
- May have removed one limitation too aggressively

- Improves polish and maintainability

**Cons**

2. clarify that `golang-github-nvidia-container-toolkit` is the validated package for the current Fedora-based Podman machine
3. consider restoring “Large-model performance” to Current Limitations unless intentionally removed

## Suggested cleanup wording

For the package note, something like:

> In the current Fedora-based Podman machine environment, install the toolkit with:

followed by the existing command.

That preserves your validated evidence while signaling version sensitivity.

## Bottom line

**Verdict:** good commit, technically aligned with the tested workflow, worth keeping.  
**Recommended next step:** one small cleanup pass for clarity and version-awareness.

```
    ▆
 1. └─utils::download.file(...)
```
Thanks I made the Option C change. Now I'd like to move on to configuring Ollama to work inside Continue. When I'm trying to download, I get this error: 

## Copilot

The error is a **Windows TLS certificate revocation check failure in curl/Schannel**, not a Continue-specific problem. Your `download.file(..., method = "curl")` call is invoking curl on Windows, and Schannel is failing with `CRYPT_E_REVOCATION_OFFLINE`, meaning Windows could not contact the certificate revocation server. R’s `download.file` documentation notes that on Windows with Schannel/libcurl, revocation checks can fail this way, and `R_LIBCURL_SSL_REVOKE_BEST_EFFORT=TRUE` is a documented workaround with reduced security. ([stat.ethz.ch](https://stat.ethz.ch/R-manual/R-devel/library/utils/html/download.file.html?utm_source=openai))

Here are the **best options**, ranked.

### Option 1 — Switch from `method = "curl"` to PowerShell/`libcurl`
**What it is:** avoid the specific external curl+Schannel path that is failing.

**Pros**
- Lowest-friction fix
- Usually enough in corporate/offline-revocation environments
- Keeps the workflow mostly the same

**Cons**
- May still hit revocation/proxy issues depending on environment

### Option 2 — Use `libcurl` with best-effort revocation
**What it is:** set `R_LIBCURL_SSL_REVOKE_BEST_EFFORT=TRUE` and use `download.file(method = "libcurl")`.

**Pros**
- Specifically documented by R for this Windows revocation failure
- Good workaround when revocation servers are unreachable

**Cons**
- Slightly reduced TLS revocation strictness
- Should be documented as a workstation-network workaround, not a default best practice

### Option 3 — Download the VSIX outside R, then install locally
**What it is:** use a browser or PowerShell to fetch the `.vsix`, then install with `positron --install-extension`.

**Pros**
- Most robust operational workaround
- Easy to explain in docs as fallback

**Cons**
- Less automated
- Two-step workflow

### Option 4 — Fix the network/proxy/certificate environment
**What it is:** make the workstation able to reach revocation endpoints.

**Pros**
- Best underlying fix
- Helps other tools too

**Cons**
- Usually requires IT/network changes
- Not the fastest path

## My recommendation

Use **Option 1 first**, then **Option 2** if needed.

## Try this first in R

Replace `method = "curl"` with `method = "libcurl"`:

    download.file(
      url = latest_version_url,
      destfile = downloaded_vsix,
      method = "libcurl",
      mode = "wb"
    )

R documents `libcurl` as the modern/default path, and on Windows it uses OS-provided trust when linked with Schannel. ([stat.ethz.ch](https://stat.ethz.ch/R-manual/R-devel/library/utils/html/download.file.html?utm_source=openai))

## If that still fails, use the documented workaround

In the same R session:

    Sys.setenv(R_LIBCURL_SSL_REVOKE_BEST_EFFORT = "TRUE")

    download.file(
      url = latest_version_url,
      destfile = downloaded_vsix,
      method = "libcurl",
      mode = "wb"
    )

R’s manual explicitly says that on Windows with Schannel, failures can occur when revocation cannot be established, and that `R_LIBCURL_SSL_REVOKE_BEST_EFFORT=TRUE` can change that behavior, with reduced security. ([stat.ethz.ch](https://stat.ethz.ch/R-manual/R-devel/library/utils/html/download.file.html?utm_source=openai))

## If you want the most reliable Windows fallback

Use PowerShell to download, then install from the saved file.

### In PowerShell
    $url = "https://open-vsx.org/api/Continue/continue/win32-x64/1.3.38/file/Continue.continue-1.3.38@win32-x64.vsix"
    $dest = "$env:TEMP\Continue.continue-1.3.38.vsix"
    Invoke-WebRequest -Uri $url -OutFile $dest
    positron --install-extension $dest

This avoids the exact R `download.file(method="curl")` path that failed. The Open VSX registry currently lists Continue 1.3.38, so that version target is consistent with your doc snippet. ([open-vsx.org](https://open-vsx.org/?utm_source=openai))

## Best doc implication for `ai-ollama-dev.qmd`

Your current Continue install snippet is fragile on corporate Windows machines because it hard-codes:

- `method = "curl"`

I would recommend changing that section to either:
1. use `method = "libcurl"` instead, or
2. provide a PowerShell fallback immediately below it.

## Recommended replacement strategy

### Highest-confidence doc change
Change:

    method = "curl",
    extra = "-L"

to:

    method = "libcurl"

because `download.file` handles redirects itself for libcurl, and this avoids the exact failing path you hit. This recommendation is grounded in R’s official `download.file` documentation and Windows behavior notes. ([stat.ethz.ch](https://stat.ethz.ch/R-manual/R-devel/library/utils/html/download.file.html?utm_source=openai))

### Add a fallback note
Something like:

- If the download fails on Windows with certificate revocation or proxy errors, download the `.vsix` with PowerShell and install it manually.

## Minimal next step

Try this exact R code:

    downloaded_vsix <- tempfile("Continue.continue-1.3.38", fileext = ".vsix")

    download.file(
      url = latest_version_url,
      destfile = downloaded_vsix,
      method = "libcurl",
      mode = "wb"
    )

If that fails, then run:

    Sys.setenv(R_LIBCURL_SSL_REVOKE_BEST_EFFORT = "TRUE")

    download.file(
      url = latest_version_url,
      destfile = downloaded_vsix,
      method = "libcurl",
      mode = "wb"
    )

If you want, I can next give you **3–5 repo-ready documentation options** for the Continue installation subsection, ranked by confidence, and wait for your selection.

## Copilot

Great — that confirms the root cause was the **Windows revocation-check path**, and that **`libcurl` plus `R_LIBCURL_SSL_REVOKE_BEST_EFFORT=TRUE`** works in your environment.

## What this means

You now have direct evidence that:

- `method = "curl"` is not reliable in your Windows environment
- `method = "libcurl"` works
- the revocation best-effort setting is needed, at least on this workstation/network

That gives us a strong basis for a documentation update.

## Best options for the manual

### Option 1 — Replace `method = "curl"` with `method = "libcurl"` and add a fallback note
**What it is:** keep the R-based install flow, but use the more reliable method and document the revocation workaround only if needed.

**Pros**
- Minimal change
- Keeps the workflow simple
- Best balance of cleanliness and practicality

**Cons**
- Some users may still need the environment variable workaround

### Option 2 — Bake the environment variable into the primary example
**What it is:** make the working workaround part of the default documented code block.

**Pros**
- Highest chance of success for similar Windows environments
- Most reproducible for your current user base

**Cons**
- Slightly weaker security posture by default
- Should be documented carefully as a network/workstation compatibility workaround

### Option 3 — Keep the R example, but add PowerShell as the preferred Windows fallback
**What it is:** present R install first, then a manual PowerShell fallback if the download fails.

**Pros**
- Robust
- Good for supportability

**Cons**
- More verbose
- Two paths to maintain

## My recommendation

I recommend **Option 1**, with a short **Windows fallback note** that includes the `R_LIBCURL_SSL_REVOKE_BEST_EFFORT` workaround.

That best fits the repo instructions:
- authoritative,
- minimal assumptions,
- reproducible,
- no overcommitting to a less secure default.

## Suggested wording strategy

### Primary example
Use `method = "libcurl"` instead of `method = "curl"`.

### Fallback note
Add a short note like:

- On some Windows workstations, certificate revocation checks may prevent the download from succeeding.
- If this occurs, set `R_LIBCURL_SSL_REVOKE_BEST_EFFORT=TRUE` for the current R session and retry the download.

## Important detail

If you use `method = "libcurl"`, the `extra = "-L"` argument is not really the key fix and may be unnecessary. The working change here is the switch away from external `curl` plus the revocation setting.

So I would not keep `extra = "-L"` unless you have a specific reason to retain it.

## Recommended replacement snippet

Here is the **best candidate** for the chapter’s Continue install example:

    ## Install Positron Extensions
    latest_version_url <- "https://open-vsx.org/api/Continue/continue/win32-x64/1.3.38/file/Continue.continue-1.3.38@win32-x64.vsix"
    latest_version_filename <- basename(file.path(latest_version_url))
    
    downloaded_vsix <- tempfile(latest_version_filename, fileext = ".vsix")
    
    download.file(
      url = latest_version_url,
      destfile = downloaded_vsix,
      method = "libcurl",
      mode = "wb"
    )
    
    system(
      paste("positron --install-extension", shQuote(downloaded_vsix))
    )

And then add a short fallback note:

    On some Windows workstations, certificate revocation checks may prevent the download from succeeding. If this occurs, run:
    
        Sys.setenv(R_LIBCURL_SSL_REVOKE_BEST_EFFORT = "TRUE")
    
    Then retry the `download.file()` step.

## Why I would not make the environment variable the default yet

You have evidence it was needed on **your** workstation, but not yet evidence it is needed for **all** target environments. So it is better documented as:
- a known Windows workaround,
- not the universal first-line default.

That is more precise and more maintainable.

## Next step

Per the repo’s review-first rules, here are the top 3 documentation choices:

1. **Recommended:** `libcurl` default + Windows workaround note  
2. `libcurl` default + PowerShell fallback section  
3. `libcurl` + `R_LIBCURL_SSL_REVOKE_BEST_EFFORT=TRUE` in the main example

Pick one, and I’ll draft the exact replacement text for the Continue install subsection in paste-block style.

## Now, moving on to configuring Continue

Given your repo instructions, here are **4 ranked options** for the Continue configuration section.

### Option 1 — Keep the current minimal YAML example, but add a verification checklist
**What it is:** retain the simple local Ollama config and add clear tests for:
- model listing,
- successful chat response,
- no network errors.

**Pros**
- Small change
- Low maintenance
- Good next step

**Cons**
- Doesn’t address config drift in newer Continue versions
- Slightly underspecified

### Option 2 — Expand the section into a task-oriented “Configure and verify Continue with local Ollama” workflow
**What it is:** split into:
1. open Continue config,
2. add model,
3. verify connection,
4. test with selected code.

**Pros**
- Best for user-manual clarity
- Better information architecture
- Stronger verification trail

**Cons**
- More verbose

### Option 3 — Add version-sensitivity language around Continue config schema
**What it is:** keep the example but explicitly say the schema may change and users should check current Continue docs if fields differ.

**Pros**
- More robust
- Honest about ecosystem drift

**Cons**
- Still leaves the section relatively thin

### Option 4 — Add both Ollama API verification and Continue-side verification before showing the YAML
**What it is:** make the user prove the local endpoint first, then configure Continue.

- Minimizes false blame on Continue

**Cons**

## My recommendation

I recommend **Option 2 + Option 3**:
- make the Continue section more task-oriented,
- keep the example concise,
- add a short note that Continue configuration schema may evolve.

That best fits the repo’s user-manual and reviewability goals.

## Suggested replacement text

Below is **paste-block style** text for the Continue-related sections only.

Part 1 of 1 — replace `## Install Continue`, `## Configure Continue`, and `## Test Continue`

    ## Install Continue
    
    Open Positron, open the Extensions panel, and search for `Continue`.
    
    If the extension is available in the Extensions panel, install it there.
    
    If the extension is not available in the Extensions panel, use the manual installation method below.
    
    This workflow has been validated on a Windows workstation using a portable user-level Positron installation suitable for IT-managed environments without administrator access.
    
    ```r
    ## Install Positron Extensions
    latest_version_url <- "https://open-vsx.org/api/Continue/continue/win32-x64/1.3.38/file/Continue.continue-1.3.38@win32-x64.vsix"
    latest_version_filename <- basename(file.path(latest_version_url))
    
    downloaded_vsix <- tempfile(latest_version_filename, fileext = ".vsix")
    
    download.file(
      url = latest_version_url,
      destfile = downloaded_vsix,
      method = "libcurl",
      mode = "wb"
    )
    
    system(
      paste("positron --install-extension", shQuote(downloaded_vsix))
    )
    ```
    
    On some Windows workstations, certificate revocation checks may prevent the download from succeeding. If this occurs, run:
    
    ```r
    Sys.setenv(R_LIBCURL_SSL_REVOKE_BEST_EFFORT = "TRUE")
    ```
    
    Then retry the `download.file()` step above.
    
    Restart Positron if prompted.
    
    Continue may take about a minute to initialize the first time Positron starts after installation.
    
    ## Configure Continue
    
    Configure Continue to use the local Ollama service.
    
    On the validated Windows workstation configuration, the Continue configuration file is located at:
    
        C:/Users/<username>/.continue/config.yaml
    
    A minimal local Ollama configuration that has been validated for this workflow is:
    
    ```yaml
    name: local-ollama
    
    models:
      - name: qwen3
        provider: openai
        apiBase: http://localhost:11434/v1
        apiKey: dummy
        model: qwen3:8b
    ```
    
    This configuration uses Ollama's local OpenAI-compatible API endpoint.
    
    The Continue configuration format may change over time. If the schema differs in a future release, consult the current Continue documentation and adapt the equivalent local Ollama settings.
    
    ## Test Continue
    
    Open Continue inside Positron.
    
    Confirm that:
    
    * Continue opens successfully.
    * The configured local model is available.
    * No network or authentication errors are displayed.
    
    Start with a simple prompt to confirm that Continue can reach the local Ollama endpoint.
    
    For example:
    
    > Reply with the single word: connected
    
    Then test Continue against the active file context.
    
    In a source file, ask Continue:
    
    > Describe `@Current File`
    
    A successful result should confirm that:
    
    * Continue can communicate with the local Ollama service.
    * Continue can access editor context.
    * The configured local model responds correctly inside Positron.
