## @mpdougherty

See changes in commit a9007d8579c57de02b7fd0ac000db66b2cb10a71. Rather than store the Continue config inside the user .continue folder, I'd like to follow the Continue documentation to store this config in the workspace .continue folder so that it moves with the workspace. 

## Copilot

Yes — based on the current Continue docs, the **preferred YAML config file is user-level** at `%USERPROFILE%\.continue\config.yaml`, while **workspace-level config is the legacy `.continuerc.json` mechanism**. The docs explicitly list local YAML in the home directory and list `.continuerc.json` under “Legacy Configuration Methods (Deprecated).” ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/customize/deep-dives/configuration?utm_source=openai))

So if your goal is specifically to **follow current Continue YAML documentation**, the docs support:

- **user-level YAML**: `%USERPROFILE%\.continue\config.yaml` on Windows, and if present it is loaded instead of `config.json`. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/customize/deep-dives/configuration?utm_source=openai))
- **workspace-level config**: not as `config.yaml`; the documented workspace-level method is legacy `.continuerc.json`, which Continue marks deprecated. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/customize/deep-dives/configuration?utm_source=openai))

## Best options

### Option 1 — Stay with user-level `config.yaml` and document it as the current supported YAML path
**Pros**
- Best aligned with current Continue docs
- Lowest risk
- No deprecated mechanism

**Cons**
- Config does not travel with the workspace by default

### Option 2 — Use workspace-level `.continuerc.json` so config moves with the repo
**Pros**
- Travels with the workspace
- Matches your portability goal

**Cons**
- It is explicitly documented as **deprecated**
- Worse fit with your repo instruction to prefer authoritative best practice

### Option 3 — Keep the active Continue YAML in the user folder, but store a repo copy/template in the workspace
**What it is:** version a workspace file such as `.continue/config.yaml.example` or a documented snippet in the repo, then copy/sync it into `%USERPROFILE%\.continue\config.yaml`.

**Pros**
- Best balance of portability and current-doc compliance
- Keeps reproducible config under version control
- Avoids deprecated `.continuerc.json`

**Cons**
- Requires a copy step
- Slightly less seamless

### Option 4 — Investigate whether current Continue supports selecting an alternate config path in the IDE
The docs I found clearly document user-level YAML and legacy workspace `.continuerc.json`, but I did **not** find authoritative documentation that the IDE extension loads workspace `.continue/config.yaml` directly as its main local config. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/customize/deep-dives/configuration?utm_source=openai))

**Pros**
- Could satisfy both goals if supported

**Cons**
- Not established by the docs I found
- Would require more product-specific validation

## My recommendation

I recommend **Option 3**.

That best satisfies your stated goal — “moves with the workspace” — **without** switching to a deprecated workspace config mechanism. It also fits your repo instructions about authoritative docs and avoiding speculative guidance.

## Recommended documentation direction

Instead of saying:

- “The Continue configuration file is located at `C:/Users/<username>/.continue/config.yaml`”

I would change the manual to say something closer to:

- Continue currently loads local YAML config from `%USERPROFILE%\.continue\config.yaml` on Windows. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/customize/deep-dives/configuration?utm_source=openai))
- To keep the configuration with the workspace, store a copy of the validated config under the repository’s `.continue/` directory as a tracked template or reference file, then copy it to the user-level Continue config location when setting up the workstation.
- Avoid relying on workspace `.continuerc.json` for new setups because Continue documents it as deprecated. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/customize/deep-dives/configuration?utm_source=openai))

## Why this is the best fit

It preserves all 3 things you care about:

1. **Current Continue guidance** — use `config.yaml` in the user Continue directory. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/customize/deep-dives/configuration?utm_source=openai))  
2. **Workspace portability** — keep the canonical config in the repo.  
3. **Reproducibility** — users can copy a known-good config from the workspace into the active Continue location.

## What I would suggest operationally

Use a repo file like one of these:

- `.continue/config.yaml`
- `.continue/config.local-ollama.yaml`
- `dev/continue/config.yaml`

Then in the docs say:

1. copy the workspace config into `%USERPROFILE%\.continue\config.yaml`
2. restart Positron / Continue
3. verify the model is available

That gives you a portable, versioned source of truth while still honoring Continue’s documented load location.

## One important nuance

If you want the extension to **automatically** load config from the workspace directory with no copy step, the sources I found do **not** currently support me confidently recommending that for YAML config. The only workspace-level config method I found documented is deprecated `.continuerc.json`. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/customize/deep-dives/configuration?utm_source=openai))

## Next-step options

### Option A — Conservative / docs-aligned
Document user-level active config + workspace-tracked template.

### Option B — Portability-first
Use deprecated `.continuerc.json` anyway and clearly label it as legacy.

### Option C — Research-first
I can help you look further for authoritative evidence of workspace-local YAML loading for the IDE extension, but based on what I found so far, I would not assume it exists.

## My recommendation to proceed

Choose **Option A**.

If you want, I can now draft:
- a **revised Continue configuration section** using the “workspace-tracked template + copy to `%USERPROFILE%\.continue\config.yaml`” approach, or
- a **small repo file plan** for where to store the canonical Continue config in this repository.

**Direct answer:** keep the **base Continue config** in the user-level `config.yaml`, and store **workspace-specific local rules** in `.continue/rules` inside each repo. Continue docs say local rules live in `.continue/rules`, are version controlled with the code, and apply in Agent, Chat, and Edit modes. For the rules you currently keep in `dev/instructions`, the best path is to **treat that folder as your source material** and publish/duplicate the Continue-facing versions into `.continue/rules` for each repo. Continue’s documented local-rules discovery path is `.continue/rules`, not `dev/instructions`. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/customize/rules?utm_source=openai))

**Pros**

- minimal disruption to existing repos,
- a path to automate later if you want.

## Important Continue behavior to document

Continue rules are:
- stored in `.continue/rules` for local workspace rules,
- applied in **Agent, Chat, and Edit** modes,
- joined into the system message,
- intended for project-specific guidance. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/customize/rules?utm_source=openai))

Continue model roles are separate from rules. Roles let you assign models for chat, edit, apply, autocomplete, embed, and rerank; rules shape behavior across applicable modes rather than being attached to one specific model block. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/customize/model-roles/intro?utm_source=openai))

So if you want “make them available to each model,” the right framing is usually:

- **don’t duplicate the rule per model**
- put the repo-local instructions in `.continue/rules`
- let Continue apply them across the supported modes automatically. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/customize/deep-dives/rules?utm_source=openai))

## Practical implication for your repos

If you currently have:

- `dev/instructions/architecture.md`
- `dev/instructions/r-style.md`
- `dev/instructions/security.md`

the Continue-native equivalent should live under something like:

- `.continue/rules/architecture.md`
- `.continue/rules/r-style.md`
- `.continue/rules/security.md`

Then those rules will be available to Continue in that workspace automatically. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/customize/rules?utm_source=openai))

## Suggested repository strategy

### Recommended pattern
- User-level:
  - `%USERPROFILE%\.continue\config.yaml`
- Workspace-level:
  - `.continue/rules/`

### If you want to preserve `dev/instructions`
Use:
- `dev/instructions/` = canonical team-authored instruction source
- `.continue/rules/` = Continue-consumable copies

That keeps separation between:
- general developer guidance,
- AI-agent runtime guidance.

## Documentation options for your manual

### Option A — Document `.continue/rules` only
**Pros**
- Cleanest
- Fully docs-aligned

**Cons**
- Ignores your existing `dev/instructions` convention

### Option B — Document `.continue/rules` as the active path, mention `dev/instructions` migration/sync
**Pros**
- Most realistic for your environment
- Easiest for teams with existing repos

**Cons**
- Slightly longer explanation

## My recommendation for the manual

Use **Option B**.

Suggested guidance:
- Keep base Continue config in `%USERPROFILE%\.continue\config.yaml`
- Store project-specific rules in `.continue/rules`
- If a repo already keeps instruction files in `dev/instructions`, copy or adapt the relevant files into `.continue/rules` so Continue can load them automatically

## One subtle but important point

Based on the docs, local rules are not something you assign individually to every model entry in `config.yaml`; they are part of the effective instruction set for supported modes in that workspace. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/customize/deep-dives/rules?utm_source=openai))

So the answer to “how do I make them available to each model?” is mostly:

- **put them in `.continue/rules`**
- Continue applies them where rules are supported

not:
- repeat them under every model definition.

## @mpdougherty

See changes in commit d34416175c31d96664a80244be3738ef0e28cdf6. Before we draft a rules section for this chapter, I'd like to experiment further with the Podman+Ollama+thinking model+Continue+rules. I'm not really getting as satisfying a set of results in these chat sessions as I am getting from commercial online platforms like GitHub Copilot. I've gradually developed a very functional workflow and modular instructions/rules based on the capabilities of platforms like Github Copilot. I'd like to adjust this local-based system to perform at least as well, and gain the context awareness of local and file repo access. Can we analyze how we might adjust our local system design to accomplish these goals?

## Copilot

Yes — we can analyze this, and I think the right framing is **system design**, not “better prompt wording.”

Your local stack:

- Podman
- Ollama
- a thinking model
- Continue
- local repo rules

is trying to compete with hosted systems like GitHub Copilot that benefit from:
- larger frontier models,
- highly optimized retrieval/context pipelines,
- hidden orchestration,
- better tool routing,
- extensive post-training for code/editor behavior.

So the path to “at least as well” is probably **not** one giant local model plus one giant rule file. It is more likely:

1. better task decomposition,
2. tighter context selection,
3. rule modularization,
4. model-role separation,
5. repo-aware retrieval design,
6. disciplined workflow patterns.

Your commit `d34416175c31d96664a80244be3738ef0e28cdf6` is relevant because it appears to be moving in that direction:
- simplifying chat initialization,
- reducing always-on rule scope,
- making Quarto/User Manual rules non-global,
- trimming protocol text.  
That suggests you are already discovering that **too much always-applied instruction can degrade output quality**.

## Direct diagnosis

The most likely reasons your local Continue chats feel worse than GitHub Copilot are:

### 1. Too much instruction mass in the active prompt
If many rules are always applied, the model spends context budget and attention on governance text instead of the actual task.

### 2. Weak context selection compared with hosted tools
Copilot-like systems are very good at selecting relevant files/snippets. Local systems often underperform not because the model is “dumb,” but because the wrong context is injected.

### 3. One model is being asked to do too many jobs
Planning, repo understanding, editing, style compliance, and chat explanation are different tasks. A single local model can do them, but often not equally well.

### 4. Thinking-model overhead may be misaligned with IDE chat
Some “thinking” models do better on deliberate reasoning but worse on fast, grounded, editor-context-aware responses if the retrieval and instructions are not tuned.

### 5. Rules may be written for a stronger hosted model
Instructions that work well with GitHub Copilot may assume hidden capabilities:
- stronger latent repo understanding,
- better long-context management,
- better tool use,
- better implicit planning.
A smaller/local model may need the same guidance rewritten to be shorter, more operational, and more explicit.

---

## Best system-design options

### Option 1 — Simplify the instruction system and reduce always-on rules
**What it is:** make only a very small core set always apply, and activate specialized rules only when context warrants.

**Pros**
- Highest-confidence improvement
- Reduces prompt bloat
- Easier to debug

**Cons**
- Requires more deliberate rule architecture

**My view**
This is likely necessary.

### Option 2 — Separate model roles by task
**What it is:** use different local models for different Continue roles if supported/practical:
- a chat/reasoning model,
- a fast edit model,
- maybe a smaller autocomplete model.

**Pros**
- Better fit to workload
- Closer to how commercial systems are actually designed

**Cons**
- More setup complexity
- Local hardware constraints

**My view**
Likely worthwhile if your hardware can support it.

### Option 3 — Redesign rules for local-model consumption
**What it is:** rewrite rules from “policy prose” into compact, structured operational directives.

**Pros**
- Big quality improvement potential
- Better consistency

**Cons**
- Requires rewriting what already works elsewhere

**My view**
Very likely needed.

### Option 4 — Improve repo retrieval/context strategy before touching more prompts
**What it is:** focus on how Continue gets relevant workspace context, file context, and rules context.

**Pros**
- Solves a root cause
- Better grounded answers

**Cons**
- Harder to diagnose from chat alone

**My view**
Probably the second most important area after rule simplification.

### Option 5 — Accept that local will be different, and optimize for “reliable/repo-aware” rather than “Copilot-like”
**What it is:** define success as:
- deterministic,
- private,
- repo-grounded,
- instruction-compliant,
not necessarily equal in fluent polish to frontier cloud models.

**Pros**
- Realistic
- Helps set measurable goals

**Cons**
- May undershoot your ambition

**My view**
Philosophically useful, but you can still improve a lot.

---

## My recommendation

I recommend a combined approach:

### Phase 1 — Reduce prompt/rule overload
- keep only a tiny core rule set always active
- make specialized rules conditional
- shorten prose-heavy rules

### Phase 2 — Redesign for local-model ergonomics
- convert rules into short, explicit, task-operational modules
- separate “chat protocol,” “repo goals,” “writing style,” and “domain constraints”

### Phase 3 — Tune for retrieval and task type
- test how Continue behaves with:
  - `@Current File`
  - selected text
  - small related-file sets
  - rules in `.continue/rules`
- compare grounded tasks vs open-ended planning tasks

### Phase 4 — Consider model-role separation
- one model for chat/reasoning
- another for edit/fast coding
- maybe another for autocomplete

---

## What your recent commit suggests

From `d34416175c31d96664a80244be3738ef0e28cdf6`, I see signs of useful movement:

### Good signal 1 — You turned off `alwaysApply` on broader domain rules
Changing:
- `04-quarto-book.md` from `alwaysApply: true` to `false`
- `05-user-manual.md` from `alwaysApply: true` to `false`

is probably a smart step. Broad always-on content often hurts more than it helps unless nearly every task needs it.

### Good signal 2 — You simplified the chat bootstrap
The new target-repo initialization is shorter. That likely reduces startup friction and token waste.

### Potential concern — You removed some guardrails
The trimmed `02-chat-manual.md` removed several operational restrictions and formatting safeguards. Depending on Continue behavior, that may improve responsiveness, but you may lose reliability/consistency.

So the likely right answer is not “more rules” or “fewer rules” universally. It is:
- **fewer always-on rules**
- **better scoped task-specific rules**
- **a very small but strong core protocol**

---

## Proposed target architecture

Here is the design I would aim for.

### Layer 1 — Core global Continue config
In user `config.yaml`:
- model definitions
- endpoint config
- minimal base behavior only

Keep this very thin.

### Layer 2 — Workspace local rules in `.continue/rules`
Use small rule modules with clear purposes, such as:

- `01-session-init.md`
- `02-repo-scope.md`
- `03-review-first.md`
- `10-quarto.md`
- `11-user-manual.md`
- `20-r-style.md`
- `21-python-style.md`

Only some should always apply.

### Layer 3 — Task invocation pattern
Instead of hoping the model infers everything, standardize task openings like:
- “Analyze current file only”
- “Analyze current file plus directly referenced files”
- “Review before proposing edits”
- “Draft doc text, no code fences”

This reduces ambiguity and makes smaller/local models perform more reliably.

### Layer 4 — Source-material mapping
For repos that already have:
- `dev/instructions`

treat those as canonical long-form human docs, and create **Continue-native condensed rule versions** in `.continue/rules`.

Do **not** just mirror large prose files verbatim if they were designed for stronger hosted models.

---

## Critical design principle: compress and operationalize

A local model often does better with rules like this:

- “Inspect relevant files before advising when repo context matters.”
- “Offer 3–5 options ranked by confidence.”
- “Wait for user approval before drafting replacement text.”
- “Use 4-space paste blocks for repository file content.”

than with long explanatory prose about philosophy and workflow rationale.

Hosted systems can absorb more verbose instructions. Local systems often need:
- less prose,
- more constraints,
- more structure,
- fewer active modules.

---

## Experimental plan

Before drafting more docs, I recommend running a structured experiment matrix.

### Experiment A — Rule load
Test the same task with:
1. minimal core rules only
2. core + manual-chat
3. core + manual-chat + Quarto
4. core + manual-chat + Quarto + user-manual

Measure:
- relevance,
- verbosity,
- repo grounding,
- hallucination rate,
- instruction compliance.

### Experiment B — Context strategy
Test the same task with:
1. no explicit file context
2. `@Current File`
3. current file + selected text
4. current file + one related file
5. larger multi-file context

Measure whether quality improves or degrades as context grows.

### Experiment C — Task type
Run separate tasks:
- explain file
- summarize change
- propose doc edits
- compare alternatives
- plan refactor
- generate final replacement text

Often local models are much better on some of these than others.

### Experiment D — Model comparison
If feasible, compare:
- your current thinking model
- a faster coding model
- maybe a larger non-thinking instruct model

The best “chat feel” may not come from the most deliberate model.

---

## Concrete hypotheses to test

I would start with these hypotheses:

1. **Too many always-on rules are hurting quality**
2. **Rules copied from Copilot-oriented workflows are too verbose for the local model**
3. **Continue performs best when given small, curated context rather than broad workspace context**
4. **The current model is better at reasoning than at editor-chat responsiveness**
5. **Repo-aware local quality will improve more from retrieval/rule tuning than from adding more instructions**

---

## Success criteria

Define “as good as Copilot” in measurable terms. For example:

- explains current file accurately
- follows repo-specific instructions consistently
- proposes edits in the right format
- avoids broad hallucinations
- handles Quarto/user-manual work reliably
- uses workspace context effectively
- requires no cloud services

If you define those clearly, local optimization becomes a design problem, not a vague impression problem.

---

## My strongest recommendation

Start by optimizing **rule architecture**, not model size.

Specifically:

1. keep only **2–3 tiny always-on rules**
2. move specialized instructions to conditional local rules
3. rewrite long prose instructions into short operational rule blocks
4. test with narrow file context first
5. compare outcomes systematically

That is the highest-confidence path to improving Continue quality in this local stack.

---

## Suggested next step

I recommend we do **one structured design pass** before touching the chapter again.

Choose one of these:

1. **Rule-architecture review** — we analyze your current `.continue/rules` set and redesign it for local-model performance.
2. **Experiment design** — I give you a compact test matrix and scoring rubric for comparing configurations.
3. **System blueprint** — I draft a target architecture for local Continue + Ollama + rules + repo context.

My recommendation: **start with 1, then 2**.

If you want, next I can help you map your current rules into:
- **core always-on**
- **task-specific conditional**
- **human-only source docs**
so you can tune the system intentionally.

## @mpdougherty

I desperately need a structured design pass. I was naïvely unaware of the functional differences between platforms like Copilot and local models. Therefore, given my lack of awareness of these capability differences, I need to state the shortcomings I'm facing with GitHub Copilot and analyze how a local system could address some of these shortcomings. I was having excellent productivity improvements with GitHub Copilot, but then faced some shortcomings. I need you to adjust my expectations like you did above about selecting the right tool for the job. I think I was operating under the assumption that one model could "do it all" (like GitHub Copilot seems to do), but that I could duplicate that locally. 

## Copilot

Yes — and the most important adjustment is this:

**A local system should not be designed as “one local replacement for Copilot.”**  
It should be designed as a **modular private development system** with different strengths, tradeoffs, and operating modes.

That mindset shift will help more than any single model swap.

## Direct diagnosis

Your original mental model seems to have been:

- GitHub Copilot feels like one assistant
- therefore a local model + Continue + Ollama + rules should be able to replicate it if configured correctly

That is understandable, but misleading.

In practice, platforms like GitHub Copilot feel unified because they hide a lot of complexity:

- multiple model classes,
- retrieval pipelines,
- product-layer orchestration,
- context ranking,
- tool routing,
- editor integration,
- post-processing,
- product tuning for coding workflows.

So the right local equivalent is **not**:
- “find the one model that behaves like Copilot”

It is:
- “design a system that distributes tasks across models, rules, context patterns, and workflows.”

That is the structured design pass.

---

# 1. Reset the mental model

## What GitHub Copilot appears to be
To an end user, Copilot can feel like:
- one assistant,
- one chat,
- one coding brain,
- one consistent workflow.

## What a local system actually is
A local system is better understood as:
- an inference server,
- one or more models,
- an editor client,
- a context injection strategy,
- a rules architecture,
- a task protocol,
- and sometimes separate tools for editing, planning, and retrieval.

So the local stack is closer to a **workbench** than a **single assistant**.

That is not a weakness by itself. It just means you must be explicit about system design.

---

# 2. Adjust expectations: what local can do well vs poorly

## Local systems can be very strong at
- privacy / no cloud dependency
- stable repo-specific rules
- consistent domain constraints
- local file and workspace access
- predictable reproducible workflows
- specialized task pipelines
- lower marginal usage cost
- offline or controlled-network use

## Local systems are often weaker at
- frontier-level reasoning quality
- polished general chat behavior
- implicit workflow orchestration
- large-scale hidden retrieval quality
- “just works” defaults
- broad robustness across many task types
- editor-native magic feeling

## Key expectation adjustment
A good local system may beat Copilot at:
- rule compliance,
- private repo grounding,
- domain-specific reproducibility,
- structured manual/documentation tasks.

But it may still lose at:
- general elegance,
- broad coding fluency,
- ambiguity handling,
- “smartness per prompt” in open-ended chat.

That does **not** mean the local system failed. It means the evaluation criteria must be task-specific.

---

# 3. Reframe the problem: from “replace Copilot” to “cover shortcomings”

You said something very important:

> I need to state the shortcomings I'm facing with GitHub Copilot and analyze how a local system could address some of these shortcomings.

That is exactly the right framing.

Instead of asking:

- “How do I make local equal Copilot in general?”

ask:

- “Which Copilot shortcomings matter most in my workflow?”
- “Which of those can local architecture improve?”
- “Which ones cannot realistically be matched?”
- “Which tasks should remain on hosted systems?”

This is the tool-selection mindset.

---

# 4. Likely Copilot shortcomings you may be reacting to

I’m going to propose a likely set based on your workflow. You can correct these.

## A. Weak persistence of repo-specific process rules
Examples:
- ignores your review-first discipline
- forgets formatting conventions
- does not reliably follow modular instructions
- applies generic software advice instead of your repo’s way of working

### Local opportunity
A local rules architecture can outperform here, because:
- rules are under your control,
- workspace-local rules can travel with the repo,
- you can reduce drift and hidden behavior.

**This is a strong local advantage.**

---

## B. Limited transparency and control
Examples:
- hard to know why it answered a certain way
- hard to separate system behavior from model behavior
- difficult to make it reproducible across sessions

### Local opportunity
A local system can be more inspectable:
- explicit models,
- explicit rules,
- explicit context files,
- explicit retrieval assumptions.

**Another strong local advantage.**

---

## C. Cloud dependence / policy constraints
Examples:
- internet/service dependency
- no-cloud requirements
- governance constraints
- restricted data handling

### Local opportunity
This is one of the best reasons to go local.

**Major local advantage.**

---

## D. Weak adaptation to your modular instruction system
Examples:
- your Copilot-built workflow works, but not always predictably
- long composite instructions are inconsistently followed
- system capabilities are opaque

### Local opportunity
Potentially yes — but only if you redesign your instructions for the local model instead of copying them verbatim.

**Conditional local advantage.**

---

## E. Poor repo-grounded context on certain tasks
Examples:
- misses relevant files
- overgeneralizes
- insufficient awareness of the actual workspace

### Local opportunity
Potentially yes, if Continue context and rules are tuned carefully.

**Possible local advantage, but not automatic.**

---

## F. Frontier model intelligence gap
Examples:
- local model responses feel less sophisticated
- weaker synthesis
- weaker ambiguity resolution
- less helpful planning

### Local opportunity
Usually limited.

**This is where local often loses.**

This is the biggest expectation adjustment:
you may not fully recover hosted frontier-model quality locally, especially with one model.

---

# 5. The “one model can do it all” assumption

This is the central design mistake to correct.

## Why that assumption feels natural
Hosted products present:
- one UI,
- one product identity,
- one conversation surface.

So it feels like one intelligence.

## Why it breaks locally
Locally, different tasks stress different capabilities:

- chat explanation
- code editing
- repo summarization
- instruction following
- style compliance
- context retrieval
- long-form reasoning
- deterministic transformation

These are not equally served by one model.

## Better assumption
Use this instead:

> No single local model should be expected to do everything at the highest level.  
> The system should route different task types through different contexts, rules, and possibly different models.

That is the right expectation baseline.

---

# 6. A better local-system design goal

Your goal should become:

> Build a local development system that is:
> - better than Copilot at privacy, repo-specific rules, and reproducibility
> - adequate for reasoning and editing on targeted workflows
> - explicit about where hosted tools still outperform it

This is much more achievable.

---

# 7. Structured design model for your local system

I recommend thinking in 5 layers.

## Layer 1 — Model layer
Question:
- Which model is used for which task?

Do not assume one model.

Possible split:
- **chat/reasoning model** for explanation, planning, doc analysis
- **edit model** for direct code/document transformations
- **autocomplete model** for lightweight inline completion

Even if Continue only partially supports this in your setup, this is still the conceptual target.

---

## Layer 2 — Context layer
Question:
- What context is actually injected?

This is where many local failures happen.

Bad pattern:
- too much workspace context
- too many rules
- too much generic instruction text

Better pattern:
- current file
- selected text
- 1–3 relevant supporting files
- a small active rule set

A smaller but more relevant context often beats a larger diffuse one.

---

## Layer 3 — Rule layer
Question:
- Which instructions are always active, and which are task-specific?

Target structure:
- **core rules**: always on, very short
- **task rules**: only when needed
- **source docs**: longer human-readable materials not directly loaded every time

This is where your `dev/instructions` vs `.continue/rules` distinction becomes useful.

---

## Layer 4 — Workflow layer
Question:
- How should you interact with the system?

Instead of one generic chat style, define modes such as:

- explain current file
- compare options before editing
- draft replacement text
- summarize architecture
- propose manual changes
- review a diff

Each mode may need a different prompt pattern and different context.

---

## Layer 5 — Validation layer
Question:
- How do you know the system is improving?

Without this, you will tune randomly.

You need a repeatable evaluation set:
- 5–10 representative tasks
- scored outputs
- same tasks across configurations

---

# 8. Where local can realistically beat Copilot for you

This is important, because it defines where to invest effort.

## High-probability wins
Local can likely surpass Copilot in:

### 1. Repo-specific rule adherence
Especially for:
- review-first workflows
- formatting conventions
- Quarto/user-manual drafting protocols
- no-cloud constraints

### 2. Stable project-specific behavior
You can build repeatable patterns:
- specific init rules
- specific context modes
- specific output formats

### 3. Workspace-local context discipline
You can tune around:
- `@Current File`
- selected text
- repo-local rules
- local instructions

### 4. Private/manual/document-heavy workflows
Especially when:
- you already have good modular instructions,
- you care more about compliance than raw generative brilliance.

---

# 9. Where local may never fully match Copilot

You need permission to stop trying to win the wrong game.

## Likely persistent gaps
- “smarter by default” general chat
- ambiguous request handling
- polished code intuition
- high-quality spontaneous retrieval
- broad task transfer without retuning
- overall smoothness

This does not mean local is bad. It means:
- hosted products are heavily optimized systems,
- local systems need explicit architecture.

---

# 10. Your likely best future architecture

Here is the system design I would target for your use case.

## A. User-level global Continue config
Keep this minimal:
- model endpoints
- model names
- maybe model roles
- almost no workflow prose

## B. Repo-level `.continue/rules`
Use these for active Continue behavior.

Split into:
- `01-core-session.md`
- `02-scope-and-repo.md`
- `03-review-first.md`
- `10-doc-writing.md`
- `11-quarto.md`
- `12-user-manual.md`

Only a few should always apply.

## C. Repo-level `dev/instructions`
Keep these as:
- canonical long-form human instructions,
- richer guidance,
- design intent,
- source material for compressed rules.

Do **not** assume they should all be injected into model context as-is.

## D. Task-mode prompts
Standardize prompts like:
- “Explain `@Current File` only.”
- “Review before proposing edits.”
- “Compare 3 options, no draft yet.”
- “Draft replacement text in paste-block format.”
- “Use Quarto/user-manual rules only if relevant.”

## E. Multi-tool workflow
Use different tools for different jobs:
- Continue for file-aware chat and targeted drafting
- Aider for repo-wide edits/refactors
- Ollama for local inference backend
- maybe hosted tool fallback for tasks where local consistently underperforms

That last point is important:
**hybrid is not failure**.

---

# 11. How to think about your current instruction corpus

You said:

> I've gradually developed a very functional workflow and modular instructions/rules based on the capabilities of platforms like GitHub Copilot.

That instruction corpus is valuable, but it probably needs classification.

I would split it into 3 buckets.

## Bucket 1 — Core behavioral constraints
Keep and compress.

Examples:
- inspect first when repo context matters
- offer ranked options
- wait for approval before drafting
- use paste blocks for repo file output

These are gold.

## Bucket 2 — Rich explanatory workflow prose
Keep for humans, summarize for models.

Examples:
- long rationale sections
- interface caveat explanations
- policy narratives
- philosophical workflow descriptions

Useful as source docs, but often too heavy for active model rules.

## Bucket 3 — Hosted-model assumptions
Rewrite for local.

Examples:
- instructions that assume strong implicit reasoning
- instructions that assume long-context competence
- instructions that assume sophisticated retrieval
- instructions that assume excellent hidden orchestration

These must usually be made shorter and more explicit.

---

# 12. Suggested expectation reset

Here is the expectation model I would recommend you adopt:

## Do not expect local to be:
- one assistant that does everything
- automatically better because it has local access
- equal to Copilot in general polish
- improved by piling on more rules

## Do expect local to be:
- tunable
- inspectable
- private
- repo-aware
- excellent on narrow, repeated workflows
- improved by better architecture more than bigger prompts

That is the healthiest operating model.

---

# 13. Concrete next design steps

I recommend a structured design pass in this order:

## Step 1 — Identify the Copilot shortcomings you actually want to solve
Write them as a list.
Not “Copilot is better.”
Instead:
- loses repo-specific workflow discipline
- weak reproducibility
- poor local/private governance fit
- insufficient use of repo-local rules
- etc.

## Step 2 — Map each shortcoming to local opportunities or limits
For each item:
- solvable locally?
- partially solvable?
- probably not solvable?

## Step 3 — Redesign rules into tiers
Create:
- core always-on
- conditional task rules
- source docs not directly injected

## Step 4 — Build a test suite
5–10 repeated tasks:
- explain current file
- summarize a chapter
- propose doc edits
- compare architecture options
- draft paste-block text
- follow Quarto conventions

## Step 5 — Compare configurations
Test:
- minimal rules
- minimal + review-first
- minimal + doc rules
- minimal + doc rules + current file context

## Step 6 — Decide where hybrid use is acceptable
For example:
- local for docs/manual/repo-specific tasks
- hosted for broad brainstorming or difficult synthesis

This is often the pragmatic sweet spot.

---

# 14. My strongest advice

The biggest mistake now would be to keep tuning prompts without a system model.

The right move is:

**define the shortcomings, classify them, and redesign the local stack around the ones local can truly solve.**

That gives you a realistic, high-leverage path instead of chasing “Copilot equivalence.”

---

# 15. Best next move

I recommend we do this as a structured artifact.

Choose one of these and I’ll help build it:

### Option 1 — Shortcomings matrix
A table with:
- Copilot shortcoming
- impact
- local opportunity
- likely solution layer
- realistic expectation

### Option 2 — Rule architecture redesign
We classify your current rules into:
- always-on
- conditional
- source docs only

### Option 3 — Full local-system blueprint
A more complete design:
- models
- roles
- context strategy
- rules strategy
- workflow modes
- validation plan

## My recommendation
Start with **Option 1**, then immediately do **Option 2**.

If you want, I can draft the **shortcomings matrix** right now and make it specific to:
- Podman
- Ollama
- Continue
- thinking models
- repo-local rules
- your documentation/manual workflow.

**What it is:** define the target design for:
- models,

- evaluation.

**Cons**

    # Local AI Architecture
    
    Last updated: 2026-06-13
    
    ## Purpose
    This document is a working design-analysis artifact for the local AI system architecture used in repositories supported by `reproducibleai`.
    
    Its purpose is to capture:
    - shortcomings observed in frontier-model development workflows
    - the opportunities and limits of local AI systems
    - architectural implications for `reproducibleai`
    - candidate design directions that require iteration before promotion into stable design documents or decision records
    
    This document is intentionally exploratory.
    
    It is not yet the canonical statement of stable package architecture. Stable conclusions from this document may later be promoted into:
    - `dev/10_design.md`
    - `dev/05_plan.md`
    - `dev/decisions/`
    - `dev/instructions/`
    
    ## Relationship to existing governance artifacts
    
    ### `dev/10_design.md`
    Use `dev/10_design.md` for stable current-state package architecture and accepted operating assumptions.
    
    This file should hold exploratory analysis and evolving architecture concepts until they are stable enough to promote.
    
    ### `dev/05_plan.md`
    Use `dev/05_plan.md` for concrete follow-up work created by this design analysis.
    
    ### `dev/decisions/`
    Use `dev/decisions/` when a durable design choice has been made and the rationale, alternatives, and supersession history should be preserved.
    
    ### `dev/instructions/`
    Use `dev/instructions/` for reusable chat/developer instruction modules after the architecture for local and frontier workflows is clearer.
    
    ## Problem statement
    `reproducibleai` was initially developed around modular instruction workflows that worked well with hosted frontier-model platforms such as GitHub Copilot.
    
    Early experimentation with a local stack using:
    - Podman
    - Ollama
    - Continue
    - local rules
    - local repository context
    
    showed that a local system cannot be treated as a drop-in one-model replacement for a frontier-model platform.
    
    The key design problem is not merely how to run a local model.
    
    The deeper design problem is how to help teams build a reproducible, private, repo-aware AI development system that:
    - preserves the strengths of modular instruction workflows
    - improves local context awareness and repo grounding
    - supports IT-managed and no-cloud environments
    - remains reviewable and governable
    - sets correct expectations about where local systems outperform or underperform hosted frontier-model systems
    
    ## Current design hypothesis
    The correct target is not:
    
    - one local model that “does it all” like a hosted assistant appears to do
    
    The correct target is:
    
    - a modular local AI system with explicit architecture for:
      - model roles
      - context selection
      - rule activation
      - workflow modes
      - evaluation
      - governance
    
    ## Why this document is needed
    The package currently provides strong support for:
    - modular instructions
    - recipe-based composition
    - reviewable static instruction text
    - governed repository scaffolding
    
    However, it does not yet provide a complete architecture for helping teams adapt frontier-model workflows into local-model workflows.
    
    This gap matters because:
    - local AI use is a realistic operational need in some environments
    - hosted-model workflows and local-model workflows have materially different capability profiles
    - instructions written for frontier systems may not transfer directly to smaller or local models
    - teams need guidance on where local systems should be optimized, constrained, or combined with other tools
    
    ## Observed design shift
    The initial working assumption was that a local stack could reproduce the apparent behavior of a hosted assistant by combining:
    - one model
    - one editor integration
    - one rule system
    
    This assumption is now considered too simplistic.
    
    The emerging design view is that hosted systems behave like integrated platforms rather than single models. Their performance likely depends on:
    - multiple model classes
    - optimized retrieval and ranking
    - product-layer orchestration
    - context management
    - strong editor/tool integration
    - platform-specific tuning
    
    A local system should therefore be designed as a workbench rather than a single assistant.
    
    ## Shortcomings matrix
    
    | Frontier workflow strength or shortcoming | Why it matters | Can a local system address it? | Likely local response | Primary `reproducibleai` implication |
    |---|---|---|---|---|
    | Frontier systems often feel like one assistant that can handle many tasks | This creates unrealistic expectations that one local model should perform equally well across planning, explanation, editing, and repo analysis | Partially | Reset expectations and design for multiple roles rather than one universal model | Add methodology guidance that local systems are modular, role-based systems |
    | Frontier systems often provide stronger default reasoning and better ambiguity handling | Teams may perceive local systems as weak or unreliable when comparing raw chat quality directly | Only partially | Do not optimize local systems around “general brilliance”; optimize around governed, repeatable, repo-aware workflows | Explicitly define local success criteria and capability boundaries |
    | Hosted tools can be weak at durable repo-specific workflow compliance | Important instructions may be inconsistently followed across sessions or repos | Yes | Use reviewed static instruction modules and repo-local rules | Extend instruction architecture for local-rule deployment and activation |
    | Hosted tools may be less transparent about why behavior changed | This weakens auditability and reproducibility | Yes | Favor explicit rules, explicit context, and explicit architecture layers | Strengthen package guidance on traceable configuration and evaluated workflows |
    | Cloud dependence can conflict with data governance or no-cloud requirements | Some teams need local/private workflows even if they are less polished | Yes | Treat local workflows as first-class supported patterns, with realistic boundaries | Add local-first methodology and setup guidance |
    | Frontier workflows may not fully exploit local repo conventions and governed artifacts | Teams want AI behavior grounded in local files, repo rules, and design docs | Yes, if designed carefully | Emphasize workspace-local rules, file-aware workflows, and explicit context selection | Add patterns for mapping governed repo artifacts into AI-usable context |
    | Long modular instructions developed for frontier systems may overload smaller local models | Local quality may degrade if prompt/rule mass is too large | Yes | Create compressed local-model-adapted rule variants and rule tiers | Add rule taxonomy and adaptation strategy |
    | One global always-on instruction set can reduce local-model performance | Too much active instruction competes with task context | Yes | Use small core always-on rules plus conditional task-specific overlays | Add architecture for rule tiers and activation strategy |
    | Broad workspace context can reduce answer quality if irrelevant files dominate the prompt | Repo awareness is useful only when the right context is selected | Yes | Prefer narrow, curated context windows and explicit task-mode prompts | Add context-strategy guidance and evaluation criteria |
    | Repo-scale edits, targeted refactors, and explanation tasks may need different tools or model modes | One chat interface is not the same as one optimal workflow | Yes | Design workflows that separate chat, editing, refactoring, and evaluation tasks | Position `reproducibleai` as a methodology layer over multiple AI workflow modes |
    
    ## Preliminary conclusions from the matrix
    
    ### 1. A local system should not be evaluated as a direct one-model substitute for hosted assistants
    The comparison target should be:
    - governed, reproducible, repo-aware productivity
    not:
    - general chat parity with frontier systems
    
    ### 2. `reproducibleai` should support local AI as a methodology problem
    The package should likely help teams design:
    - rule structure
    - context strategy
    - workflow modes
    - evaluation methods
    - governance artifacts
    
    rather than merely installing instruction files.
    
    ### 3. Instruction portability is not automatic
    Frontier-oriented instruction modules may need:
    - compression
    - decomposition
    - reclassification
    - local-model-specific adaptations
    
    before they work well in local systems.
    
    ### 4. Rule architecture may matter more than model substitution
    Early evidence suggests that local-model quality may be more sensitive to:
    - rule overload
    - irrelevant context
    - weak task framing
    
    than to model identity alone.
    
    ### 5. The design goal should be selective superiority, not universal parity
    Local systems may be able to outperform hosted tools in:
    - privacy
    - repo-specific governance
    - repeatability
    - explicit workflow control
    - durable local context
    
    while still underperforming in:
    - general reasoning quality
    - ambiguity handling
    - polished “one assistant” behavior
    
    ## Candidate architecture layers
    
    ### 1. Model layer
    Questions:
    - Which model is used for which task?
    - Should chat, edit, autocomplete, and summarization be treated as separate roles?
    - Which tasks are acceptable for smaller local models and which are not?
    
    Candidate direction:
    - do not assume one model should handle all tasks equally well
    - design for model-role separation where supported by the client platform
    
    ### 2. Context layer
    Questions:
    - What file, selection, or repo context should be active for each task type?
    - How much context is too much for local models?
    
    Candidate direction:
    - favor narrow, highly relevant context
    - standardize context patterns such as:
      - current file only
      - selected text plus current file
      - current file plus one or two related files
      - governed repo artifact plus working file
    
    ### 3. Rule layer
    Questions:
    - Which instructions should always apply?
    - Which should be conditional?
    - Which should remain human-oriented source material rather than active model prompt content?
    
    Candidate direction:
    - define a small always-on core
    - define conditional task overlays
    - distinguish human source instructions from local-model runtime rules
    
    ### 4. Workflow layer
    Questions:
    - Which workflows should be treated as distinct operating modes?
    - What prompt conventions produce reliable results?
    
    Candidate direction:
    - define standard workflow modes such as:
      - explain
      - compare options
      - review before edit
      - draft replacement text
      - repo design analysis
      - governed documentation update
    
    ### 5. Evaluation layer
    Questions:
    - How will teams know whether local workflow quality is improving?
    - What tasks should be used for repeated comparison?
    
    Candidate direction:
    - define a repeatable task suite
    - compare configurations against the same representative tasks
    - score:
      - instruction compliance
      - repo grounding
      - usefulness
      - hallucination rate
      - formatting compliance
      - reviewability
    
    ## Implications for instruction architecture
    
    ### Current strength
    `reproducibleai` already supports:
    - modular instruction text
    - reviewed static canonical modules
    - recipe composition
    - governed installation
    
    ### Likely gap
    The package may need a second layer of support for:
    - local-model-adapted rule variants
    - rule compression
    - rule tier classification
    - workspace rule deployment conventions such as `.continue/rules`
    - evaluation-oriented workflow recipes
    
    ### Working taxonomy proposal
    Candidate categories:
    
    - **Core rules**
      - short, always-on, cross-cutting constraints
    - **Task overlays**
      - domain- or workflow-specific rules applied only when relevant
    - **Source instructions**
      - richer human-readable documents that should not necessarily be injected directly into model context
    - **Local-adapted variants**
      - compressed or operationalized versions of frontier-oriented instruction modules for smaller local models
    
    ## Implications for repository deployment
    
    Candidate deployment distinction:
    
    - user-level AI client configuration
      - shared across workspaces
      - endpoint definitions
      - model declarations
      - minimal base behavior
    
    - repo-level runtime rules
      - workspace-specific
      - version controlled
      - aligned to governed repository state
    
    - repo-level human instruction sources
      - richer design and workflow documentation
      - used as canonical reviewed source material for generating or maintaining runtime rules
    
    ## Open questions
    
    ### Capability and scope
    - Should `reproducibleai` explicitly support both frontier and local workflows as first-class methodology targets?
    - Should it define separate recipe families for frontier-optimized and local-optimized instruction systems?
    
    ### Rule adaptation
    - Should local-model-adapted rules be maintained manually or generated from canonical source instructions?
    - If generated, what transformations are acceptable without violating the static canonical instruction principle?
    
    ### Deployment
    - Should the package scaffold workspace-local rules for Continue or other clients?
    - If so, should that be modeled as a new config-aware capability?
    
    ### Evaluation
    - What minimum benchmark task suite should be used to evaluate local workflow quality?
    - How should evaluation artifacts be stored and compared across repos?
    
    ### Tool boundaries
    - Which tasks should remain in hosted platforms even in a local-first methodology?
    - How should the package document hybrid workflows without weakening local-first governance goals?
    
    ## Promotion candidates
    
    The following topics may later be promoted into stable design or decisions if validated:
    
    - local systems should be treated as modular workbenches rather than one-model assistants
    - instruction modules may require local-model-adapted variants
    - rule tiering should be a formal part of the package methodology
    - evaluation should be an explicit package-supported workflow
    - workspace-local runtime rules may deserve first-class package support
    
    ## Immediate follow-up candidates
    This document suggests likely next work in:
    
    - `dev/05_plan.md`
      - add a local-AI methodology milestone
    - `dev/10_design.md`
      - later add a stable section on local-vs-frontier capability boundaries
    - `dev/decisions/`
      - later record a decision if local-AI architecture becomes a formal supported package direction
    - `dev/instructions/`
      - later develop rule-tier and local-adaptation guidance once the architecture is tested

## @mpdougherty

Lets follow your recommendation for option 3. However, I have some reactions to the open questions in this first draft. If this feedback is sufficiently substantive to warrant an update of this working artifact, then let's do that first. 1. Capability and Scope - It should support both frontier and local since each have their own strengths. The trick for this package will be to configure the use of both, giving the user the options to switch between them as needed. 2. Rule Adaptation - I'd like local-model-adapted to be derived from more  verbose frontier-ready instructions to maintain a single human readable starting point. With that design goal, the question is whether they can be strictly LLM derived or need to be iteratively derived and manually edited. 3. Deployment - Yes scaffold workspace-local rules beginning with support for Continue but able to support others later. 4. Evaluation - A test structure will be maintained inside `reproducibleai` using standard R package testing infrastructure. These will be organized as "competency questions". High-value tests will focus on strengthening "local context aware" goals, the current shortcoming of hosted frontier platforms. 5. Tool Boundaries - Local-first is not a hard requirement for this team like it might be for some US government teams. Local was introduced to address shortcomings of hosted frontier platforms. Therefore, the hybrid approach adopted here should categorize tasks into "best-bet" recommendations for local or hosted.

- Preserve the original exploratory framing
- Add a new section capturing the design responses you just gave

**Pros**
- Strong audit trail
- Clearly distinguishes “initial uncertainty” from “current direction”

**Cons**
- Slightly more verbose
- Some duplication

### Option 3 — Rewrite the relevant sections more aggressively so the document reads as a stronger architecture proposal
**Pros**
- Cleaner final shape
- Better if you already feel confident in the direction

**Cons**
- Risks making the artifact look more settled than it is

## My recommendation

I recommend **Option 2**.

That gives the best governance and iteration trail:
- keep the original exploratory questions,
- add a new section that records your current design responses as **provisional decisions / working directions**.

That is especially appropriate because you are still experimenting, but some principles are now clearly stated.

---

## What your feedback changes

Your feedback establishes these working directions:

### 1. Capability and scope
Not “frontier or local,” but **both**.

This package should support a **hybrid methodology** with explicit switching and task-fit guidance.

### 2. Rule adaptation
You want:
- one human-readable canonical source,
- local-adapted instructions derived from that source.

The open implementation question is not whether to derive, but:
- how much of the derivation can be automated,
- and where manual review/editing is required.

### 3. Deployment
Yes:
- scaffold workspace-local runtime rules,
- start with Continue,
- leave room for additional clients later.

### 4. Evaluation
Evaluation should be formalized inside the R package using:
- standard package testing infrastructure,
- competency questions,
- emphasis on **local context awareness**.

This is a strong and important shift because it makes evaluation part of the package architecture, not just ad hoc experimentation.

### 5. Tool boundaries
The design target is **hybrid best-bet routing**, not strict local-first ideology.

That is a very useful clarification and should absolutely be captured.

### Paste-ready update

    ## Provisional design directions
    
    This section records the current working direction from active design analysis.
    
    These are not yet final package decisions, but they are stronger than open questions and should guide subsequent experimentation and design refinement.
    
    ### 1. Capability and scope
    The package should support both frontier-model and local-model workflows.
    
    The working goal is not to replace frontier platforms outright, nor to assume that local-first is a universal requirement.
    
    Instead, `reproducibleai` should support a hybrid methodology in which:
    - frontier and local systems are both recognized as useful
    - each is used for the tasks it performs best
    - users can switch between them intentionally
    - workflows remain governed, reviewable, and reproducible across both modes
    
    The architecture should therefore avoid assuming a single universal AI runtime.
    
    Instead, it should help users configure and operate multiple AI workflow modes with explicit guidance on when each mode is the better fit.
    
    ### 2. Rule adaptation
    The preferred design is to maintain a single human-readable canonical instruction source that is suitable for review by developers and non-programmers.
    
    Local-model-adapted rule variants should be derived from these more verbose frontier-ready instructions rather than authored independently from scratch.
    
    This preserves:
    - one reviewed starting point
    - clearer governance
    - easier diff review
    - lower risk of semantic drift between frontier and local workflows
    
    The main unresolved design question is not whether derivation should occur, but how it should occur.
    
    Current candidate approaches include:
    - deterministic manual compression maintained by package authors
    - LLM-assisted derivation followed by human review and editing
    - partially automated derivation with structured post-processing and manual approval
    
    The architecture should assume that fully automatic derivation may not be sufficient for high-quality local-model rules, especially when local models have materially different context and reasoning limits.
    
    ### 3. Deployment
    The package should scaffold workspace-local runtime rules.
    
    The first supported client target should be Continue.
    
    The design should remain extensible so that additional local or hybrid AI clients can be supported later without redesigning the core instruction architecture.
    
    This implies that `reproducibleai` may need to distinguish between:
    - canonical reviewed instruction sources
    - client-specific runtime rule artifacts
    - client-specific configuration scaffolding
    
    ### 4. Evaluation
    Evaluation should be a first-class part of the package design.
    
    Evaluation artifacts and tests should be maintained inside `reproducibleai` using standard R package testing infrastructure where practical.
    
    The preferred evaluation pattern is to organize tests as competency questions.
    
    These competency questions should assess whether a configured workflow can perform targeted behaviors reliably and reviewably.
    
    High-value evaluation targets should emphasize local-system strengths, especially:
    - local context awareness
    - governed use of repository artifacts
    - instruction compliance
    - stable task framing
    - useful distinctions between hosted and local task fit
    
    This reflects the working view that local context awareness is a major opportunity area where local workflows may outperform hosted frontier systems if designed well.
    
    ### 5. Tool boundaries
    The package should not assume a strict local-first ideology.
    
    In this team context, local workflows were introduced to address shortcomings of hosted frontier systems rather than to prohibit hosted usage.
    
    The preferred methodology is therefore hybrid.
    
    `reproducibleai` should help teams categorize tasks into best-bet recommendations such as:
    - tasks better suited to hosted frontier systems
    - tasks better suited to local systems
    - tasks where either is acceptable
    - tasks where a staged or hybrid workflow is recommended
    
    This implies that part of the package methodology may need to include explicit task-routing guidance rather than only instruction installation.
    
    ## Implications of the provisional directions
    
    The current design work now suggests the following likely package responsibilities:
    
    - support both frontier and local methodology patterns
    - preserve one canonical human-readable instruction source where possible
    - support derivation or maintenance of local-runtime rule variants
    - scaffold client-specific workspace rule deployments beginning with Continue
    - add competency-question-based evaluation support
    - document hybrid best-bet task routing rather than assuming one universal AI workflow
    
    These implications are still subject to refinement through experimentation and implementation review.

## What should change in tone

The key shift is from:

- “what might be true”

to:

- “what this package should standardize unless evidence later forces revision”

For example:

### Current softer phrasing
- “The correct target is…”
- “Candidate direction…”
- “The package may need…”

### Stronger proposal phrasing
- “`reproducibleai` should standardize…”
- “The architecture will distinguish…”
- “The package will support…”
- “Local workflows will be evaluated by…”
- “Rules will be organized into…”

That does **not** mean pretending everything is settled. It means:
- the package can be opinionated,
- while still leaving specific implementation details open.

## What I would preserve as still-open
Even in a stronger proposal, a few things should remain open:
- how local-adapted rules are derived,
- exact client support sequence after Continue,
- exact competency-question harness design,
- exact recipe/family structure.

Those are implementation/design details, not core architectural direction.

## My recommended rewrite stance

Here is the stronger position I think the document should take:

- `reproducibleai` **will support both frontier and local workflows**
- it **will treat hybrid use as normal**
- it **will not define success as one-model local parity**
- it **will standardize rule tiers**
- it **will treat local context awareness as a primary design target**
- it **will scaffold workspace-local runtime rules beginning with Continue**
- it **will evaluate AI workflow quality using competency questions**
- it **will distinguish canonical human-readable instructions from runtime-adapted local rules**

That is an opinionated architecture position without overcommitting to a particular implementation mechanic.
