    # Local AI Architecture
    
    Last updated: 2026-06-13
    
    ## Purpose
    This document defines the proposed architecture direction for how `reproducibleai` should support AI-assisted development workflows across both frontier-model and local-model environments.
    
    `reproducibleai` is an opinionated package intended to help standardize emerging best practice for a USACE data science team. It should therefore do more than install instruction files. It should help teams adopt governed, reviewable, reproducible AI workflows that are realistic about the strengths and limits of different AI platforms.
    
    This document establishes the current architecture proposal for that work.
    
    It is stronger than exploratory notes, but not yet the final canonical statement of stable package architecture. Stable conclusions from this document may later be promoted into:
    - `dev/10_design.md`
    - `dev/05_plan.md`
    - `dev/decisions/`
    - `dev/instructions/`
    
    ## Relationship to existing governance artifacts
    
    ### `dev/10_design.md`
    Use `dev/10_design.md` for stable current-state package architecture and accepted operating assumptions.
    
    This document is the design-stage architecture proposal that will feed future updates to `dev/10_design.md` once the proposed local and hybrid workflow model has been validated.
    
    ### `dev/05_plan.md`
    Use `dev/05_plan.md` for concrete implementation work created by this architecture proposal.
    
    ### `dev/decisions/`
    Use `dev/decisions/` when a durable architecture or methodology choice from this document has been accepted and should be preserved with explicit rationale and alternatives.
    
    ### `dev/instructions/`
    Use `dev/instructions/` for reusable chat and developer instruction modules after this architecture has clarified how frontier-oriented instructions, local-adapted runtime rules, and client-specific rule artifacts should relate to one another.
    
    ## Problem statement
    `reproducibleai` was initially developed around modular instruction workflows that performed well with hosted frontier-model platforms such as GitHub Copilot.
    
    Subsequent experimentation with a local stack using:
    - Podman
    - Ollama
    - Continue
    - local rules
    - local repository context
    
    demonstrated that a local system should not be treated as a drop-in one-model replacement for a hosted frontier platform.
    
    The design problem is not merely how to run a local model.
    
    The real design problem is how to help teams build a governed AI workbench that:
    - supports both frontier and local workflows
    - preserves the strengths of modular instruction systems
    - improves local context awareness and repository grounding
    - supports IT-managed and no-cloud environments where needed
    - remains reviewable, reproducible, and governable
    - guides users toward the right tool or workflow mode for the task
    
    ## Package position
    `reproducibleai` should explicitly support both frontier-model and local-model workflows as first-class methodology targets.
    
    The package should not define success as replacing hosted assistants with one local model.
    
    Instead, it should define success as helping teams build a governed hybrid AI workflow in which:
    - frontier systems are used where they are the best fit
    - local systems are used where they are the best fit
    - repository-specific instructions and governed artifacts remain central
    - evaluation is explicit rather than anecdotal
    - architecture and workflow choices are reviewable and reproducible
    
    This package should therefore standardize methodology, structure, and evaluation more than it standardizes allegiance to any one runtime.
    
    ## Why this document is needed
    The package already provides strong support for:
    - modular instructions
    - recipe-based composition
    - reviewed static instruction text
    - governed repository scaffolding
    
    However, those strengths were established primarily in a frontier-model context.
    
    The package does not yet fully define how the same instruction-first and governance-first philosophy should operate when:
    - local models have different reasoning and context limits
    - client-specific runtime rule systems are introduced
    - users need to switch intentionally between hosted and local modes
    - evaluation must distinguish between raw model quality and system-design quality
    
    This document exists to close that gap by making the local and hybrid architecture explicit.
    
    ## Architecture position
    The package should be designed around the assumption that hosted frontier platforms behave like integrated AI systems rather than like single interchangeable models.
    
    Their apparent performance is shaped not only by model quality, but also by:
    - retrieval and ranking
    - tool orchestration
    - context management
    - editor integration
    - workflow tuning
    - hidden platform behavior
    
    A local system should therefore not be modeled as:
    - one model
    - one editor integration
    - one instruction set
    - one universal workflow
    
    A local system should instead be modeled as a governed workbench with explicit architecture for:
    - model roles
    - context selection
    - rule activation
    - workflow modes
    - evaluation
    - governance
    
    ## Observed design shift
    The earlier working assumption was that a local stack could reproduce the apparent behavior of a hosted assistant by combining:
    - one model
    - one editor integration
    - one rule system
    
    That assumption is now rejected as the core design target.
    
    The design target for `reproducibleai` should be a hybrid AI methodology that helps teams:
    - choose the right workflow mode for the task
    - deploy repository-governed instructions across different AI clients
    - adapt frontier-oriented instruction systems into local-runtime rule systems
    - evaluate AI workflow quality using explicit competency questions
    - preserve durable development governance regardless of runtime choice

Part 2 of 3 — replace the section from `## Shortcomings matrix` through the end of `## Candidate architecture layers`

    ## Shortcomings matrix
    
    | Frontier workflow strength or shortcoming | Why it matters | Can a local or hybrid system address it? | Required response from `reproducibleai` |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | Frontier systems often feel like one assistant that can handle many tasks | This creates the false expectation that one local model should perform equally well across planning, explanation, editing, and repository analysis | Only partially | Explicitly teach that AI workflows are multi-layer systems, not single-model substitutes |
    | Frontier systems often provide stronger default reasoning and better ambiguity handling | Teams may misdiagnose local-system weaknesses as “bad prompting” when the issue is actually capability mismatch or poor system design | Only partially | Define realistic success criteria for local workflows and separate architecture quality from raw model quality |
    | Hosted tools can be weak at durable repository-specific workflow compliance | Important team instructions and governed repo artifacts may not be followed consistently | Yes | Standardize repository-governed instruction systems and workspace runtime rules |
    | Hosted tools may be less transparent about why behavior changed | This weakens reproducibility, auditability, and reviewer confidence | Yes | Favor explicit architecture layers, explicit rules, and explicit evaluation |
    | Cloud dependence can conflict with governance, security, or infrastructure constraints | Teams may need local/private workflows even when hosted tools are stronger in general reasoning | Yes | Support local and hybrid workflows as first-class package targets |
    | Hosted workflows may not exploit local repository design artifacts as effectively as a tuned local system could | Teams want better use of local files, rules, schemas, plans, and design docs | Yes | Treat local context awareness as a core design target and evaluation objective |
    | Long modular instructions written for frontier systems may overload smaller local models | Local performance can degrade when instruction mass competes with task context | Yes | Introduce rule-tier architecture and local-adapted runtime rule variants |
    | Large always-on instruction sets can reduce local-model usefulness | Local systems often need smaller active prompts and more deliberate task scoping | Yes | Standardize a small core rule layer plus conditional overlays |
    | Broad workspace context can reduce answer quality when irrelevant files dominate the prompt | Context awareness is useful only when relevant context is selected well | Yes | Standardize task-specific context strategies and competency-based evaluation |
    | Explanation, editing, planning, and refactoring are not the same task | One interface should not imply one optimal model, rule set, or workflow mode | Yes | Define workflow modes and best-bet task routing rather than pretending one workflow fits all cases |
    
    ## Architecture conclusions
    The shortcomings matrix supports the following architecture conclusions.
    
    ### 1. `reproducibleai` should standardize a hybrid AI methodology
    The package should support both:
    - frontier-model workflows
    - local-model workflows
    
    It should also support intentional switching between them.
    
    The package should not assume:
    - hosted-only methodology
    - local-only methodology
    - one-model parity as the primary goal
    
    Instead, it should standardize a hybrid methodology that helps teams choose the best-bet workflow for the task while preserving governance and reproducibility.
    
    ### 2. `reproducibleai` should treat local systems as governed workbenches
    Local workflows should not be framed as one-model replacements for hosted assistants.
    
    They should be framed as governed workbenches with explicit design for:
    - model roles
    - context strategy
    - rule tiers
    - workflow modes
    - evaluation
    
    ### 3. `reproducibleai` should treat local context awareness as a major design opportunity
    Local workflows are unlikely to consistently outperform frontier systems at general reasoning quality.
    
    They may, however, outperform frontier systems in:
    - governed repository grounding
    - explicit use of local design artifacts
    - repeatable workflow compliance
    - transparent configuration
    - private and infrastructure-constrained environments
    
    For this package, local context awareness should therefore be treated as a primary optimization target rather than a secondary convenience.
    
    ### 4. `reproducibleai` should distinguish raw model capability from workflow architecture quality
    When a local workflow performs poorly, the cause may be:
    - model limitations
    - rule overload
    - weak context selection
    - poor task framing
    - wrong tool choice
    
    The package should help users evaluate and improve system design rather than treating all failures as prompt problems.
    
    ### 5. `reproducibleai` should optimize for selective superiority rather than universal parity
    The package should not promise that local workflows will match frontier platforms in every dimension.
    
    Instead, it should help teams build workflows that are selectively better in areas such as:
    - governance
    - privacy
    - reviewability
    - explicit repository grounding
    - controlled and reproducible workflow behavior
    
    ## Proposed architecture layers
    
    `reproducibleai` should standardize local and hybrid AI methodology across five architecture layers.
    
    ### 1. Model layer
    The architecture should distinguish between different model roles rather than assuming one model should do everything.
    
    The package methodology should assume that tasks such as:
    - explanation
    - planning
    - editing
    - autocomplete
    - summarization
    - repository analysis
    
    may be better served by different model roles, runtime settings, or clients.
    
    The package does not need to hard-code one universal model strategy, but it should explicitly teach model-role separation as a best practice.
    
    ### 2. Context layer
    The architecture should standardize deliberate context selection.
    
    The package should teach that better context is often more important than more context, especially for local models.
    
    Recommended context patterns should include:
    - current file only
    - selected text plus current file
    - current file plus one or two directly related files
    - working file plus governed repository artifact such as:
      - `dev/10_design.md`
      - `dev/05_plan.md`
      - `dev/40_schemas.md`
      - relevant instruction or rule files
    
    The package should discourage indiscriminate broad workspace injection as a default workflow.
    
    ### 3. Rule layer
    The architecture should standardize rule tiers.
    
    The package should distinguish between:
    - **core rules**
      - short, always-on, cross-cutting constraints
    - **task overlays**
      - domain- or workflow-specific runtime rules used only when relevant
    - **source instructions**
      - canonical human-readable reviewed instructions that serve as the source of truth
    - **local-adapted runtime rules**
      - compressed or operationalized variants derived from canonical source instructions for use in local client rule systems
    
    This distinction should become a formal part of package methodology rather than an ad hoc practice.
    
    ### 4. Workflow layer
    The architecture should standardize workflow modes instead of assuming one generic chat protocol is sufficient.
    
    Candidate workflow modes should include:
    - explain current file
    - compare options before editing
    - review before drafting
    - draft replacement text
    - analyze repository design
    - prepare governed documentation updates
    - evaluate task fit for hosted vs local execution
    
    The package should help teams define and reuse these modes consistently.
    
    ### 5. Evaluation layer
    The architecture should make evaluation a first-class concern.
    
    AI workflow quality should be evaluated with repeatable competency questions rather than anecdotal impressions alone.
    
    Evaluation should focus on questions such as:
    - does the workflow use the intended repository context correctly?
    - does it follow the applicable rules reliably?
    - does it produce useful and reviewable outputs?
    - does it choose an appropriate workflow mode for the task?
    - where does local outperform hosted, and where does it not?
    
    This evaluation model should become part of the package’s methodology and testing approach.

Part 3 of 3 — replace the section from `## Implications for instruction architecture` through the end of the file

    ## Implications for instruction architecture
    
    ### Current package strength
    `reproducibleai` already provides a strong foundation through:
    - modular instruction text
    - reviewed static canonical modules
    - recipe composition
    - governed installation into target repositories
    
    ### Required extension
    To support local and hybrid workflows well, the package should extend that foundation to include:
    - rule-tier classification
    - local-runtime rule variants derived from canonical instruction sources
    - client-specific workspace rule deployment
    - workflow-mode guidance
    - competency-question-based evaluation support
    
    ### Canonical source principle
    The package should preserve one canonical human-readable instruction source wherever possible.
    
    Frontier-ready instructions should remain the reviewed source of truth for developers and reviewers.
    
    Local-runtime rule variants should be derived from those canonical sources rather than maintained as unrelated parallel documents.
    
    This is necessary to preserve:
    - governance
    - readability
    - reviewability
    - semantic alignment across workflow modes
    
    ### Local-adapted derivation principle
    The package should treat local-rule adaptation as a governed transformation problem.
    
    The core design question is not whether local-adapted rules should exist. They should.
    
    The remaining design question is how to derive them while preserving quality and reviewer confidence.
    
    The package should therefore assume:
    - derivation from canonical human-readable instructions is the desired model
    - fully automatic derivation may not be sufficient in all cases
    - human review and iterative refinement are likely to remain necessary
    - any future automation should preserve transparent traceability to the canonical instruction source
    
    ## Implications for repository deployment
    
    The package should standardize a three-part deployment distinction.
    
    ### 1. User-level AI client configuration
    This layer should hold:
    - endpoint definitions
    - model declarations
    - client-wide defaults
    - behavior intended to apply across repositories
    
    This layer should remain minimal and should not carry the full burden of repository-specific workflow governance.
    
    ### 2. Repository-level runtime rules
    This layer should hold:
    - workspace-specific runtime rules
    - version-controlled local client artifacts
    - repository-governed behavior tied to the local project
    
    The first supported target for this layer should be Continue.
    
    The architecture should remain extensible to additional clients later.
    
    ### 3. Repository-level human instruction sources
    This layer should hold:
    - richer reviewed source instructions
    - architecture and workflow rationale
    - governance-aligned human-readable materials that should not always be injected directly into runtime prompt context
    
    This layer should be treated as the durable source material from which runtime rule artifacts may be derived or maintained.
    
    ## Proposed package direction
    
    Based on the current analysis, `reproducibleai` should move toward the following package direction:
    
    - support both frontier and local workflows as first-class methodology targets
    - document hybrid best-bet task routing rather than requiring one universal runtime
    - preserve canonical human-readable instruction sources
    - support derived local-runtime rule variants
    - scaffold workspace-local runtime rules beginning with Continue
    - formalize rule tiers as part of package methodology
    - formalize workflow modes as part of package methodology
    - formalize competency-question-based evaluation as part of package methodology
    
    ## Remaining implementation questions
    
    The major architecture direction is now clear enough to state strongly.
    
    The remaining open questions are mostly implementation questions rather than core direction questions.
    
    ### 1. Rule derivation mechanics
    Open implementation questions:
    - which parts of local-rule derivation can be automated safely
    - which parts require mandatory human editing or approval
    - how derivation lineage should be recorded
    
    ### 2. Client support sequence
    Open implementation questions:
    - what the first Continue-specific scaffolding should include
    - how client-specific artifacts should be abstracted so later clients can be supported without redesign
    
    ### 3. Evaluation harness design
    Open implementation questions:
    - how competency questions should be represented in package tests
    - what fixtures and scoring conventions should be used
    - how local-context-aware behavior should be tested consistently
    
    ### 4. Recipe and deployment structure
    Open implementation questions:
    - whether frontier-oriented and local-oriented workflows should be represented as separate recipe families
    - how hybrid workflow guidance should be exposed to users
    - how much deployment logic belongs in handlers versus supporting helpers
    
    ## Promotion targets
    
    If subsequent experimentation supports this architecture direction, the following promotions should occur:
    
    - update `dev/10_design.md` to include stable local and hybrid AI capability boundaries
    - update `dev/05_plan.md` with a formal local-AI methodology milestone
    - create a new ADR recording the decision to support hybrid frontier/local methodology with governed local runtime rule deployment
    - update `dev/instructions/` and `inst/instructions/` to reflect rule-tier and derivation-aware architecture
    
    ## Immediate follow-up work
    
    The next design and implementation work should likely include:
    
    - refining the shortcomings matrix into a more explicit task-routing framework
    - defining the first formal rule-tier taxonomy
    - defining the first Continue-specific workspace rule deployment pattern
    - designing the first competency-question evaluation structure
    - identifying which existing instruction modules need local-adapted variants first
    
    ## Current proposal status
    
    This document now serves as the package’s working architecture proposal for local and hybrid AI methodology.
    
    It should be treated as the governing design draft for this topic until its stable conclusions are promoted into:
    - `dev/10_design.md`
    - `dev/05_plan.md`
    - `dev/decisions/`

## @mpdougherty

See these changes in commit 37498286d87299f928131265ddb373ef8375625f. I choose option 1. In this next round of drafting, I'd like to compare this proposed approach to existing industry best practice approaches and how others address these concerns. I don't want to conduct an exhaustive literature review, but a quick applied research gut-check. Sanity-check: Am I going down a blind alley? What existing tooling exists to address these issues that I'd be stupid not to take advantage of? Am I unnecessarily reinventing any wheels? What package architecture changes do I need to make to leverage these best-of-breed tools?

## Copilot

Yes — this is exactly the right moment for a quick applied research gut-check, and **no, you are not obviously going down a blind alley**. But you **are** at risk of reinventing some wheels unless `reproducibleai` deliberately positions itself as a **methodology/orchestration layer over existing best-of-breed tools**, rather than trying to become a new AI client, retrieval engine, or agent runtime. Continue already gives you local workspace rules via `.continue/rules` and applies them in Agent, Chat, and Edit modes, which directly matches part of your design. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/customize/rules?utm_source=openai)) GitHub’s own Copilot guidance also explicitly frames success as choosing the right tool/task fit, adding custom instructions, and using MCP/custom agents when needed, which supports your hybrid/task-routing direction rather than contradicting it. ([docs.github.com](https://docs.github.com/en/enterprise-cloud%40latest/copilot/using-github-copilot/coding-agent/best-practices-for-using-copilot-to-work-on-tasks?utm_source=openai)) Aider’s repository map is another strong signal that repo-aware performance often depends on a **purpose-built context abstraction**, not just bigger prompts; its repo map exists specifically to provide concise whole-repo structure to the model. ([aider.chat](https://aider.chat/docs/repomap.html?utm_source=openai))

## Quick sanity check

### Are you going down a blind alley?
**No**, if the package goal is:

- standardize hybrid frontier/local best practice,
- govern instructions and runtime rules,
- scaffold repo-aware workflows,
- evaluate them with competency questions,
- and help teams choose the right runtime for the task. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/customize/rules?utm_source=openai))

### Are you going down a blind alley if you try to build everything yourself?
**Yes, potentially**, if `reproducibleai` tries to directly own:

- AI client UX,
- agent runtime orchestration,
- repo retrieval/search infrastructure,
- code-edit loop execution,
- or custom diff/edit engines.

Those wheels already exist in stronger tools like Continue and Aider. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/customize/rules?utm_source=openai))

---

## What industry best practice seems to say

Across the tooling you’re comparing, a few patterns recur:

### 1. Narrow, composable guidance beats giant universal prompts
Continue’s guidance for checks says to scope narrowly and keep one concern per check; if a rule is really just a syntax/pattern constraint, it belongs in linting rather than prompting. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/checks/best-practices?utm_source=openai))  
That strongly supports your emerging rule-tier idea:
- core runtime rules,
- task overlays,
- lint/tests for machine-checkable concerns.

### 2. Workspace-local rules are a real, mainstream pattern
Continue explicitly supports local rules in `.continue/rules`, version controlled with the code, as the project-specific mechanism. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/customize/rules?utm_source=openai))  
So your direction here is not weird; it is aligned with current practice.

### 3. “Choose the right tool for the job” is mainstream, not a fallback
GitHub’s Copilot docs explicitly emphasize understanding strengths/weaknesses and choosing the right Copilot tool/task pairing. ([docs.github.com](https://docs.github.com/en/enterprise-cloud%40latest/copilot/using-github-copilot/coding-agent/best-practices-for-using-copilot-to-work-on-tasks?utm_source=openai))  
So your hybrid best-bet routing idea is very much in line with industry practice.

### 4. Repo-aware performance often depends on retrieval/context engineering, not only model quality
Aider’s repo map is a concrete example of a specialized representation of repository structure being fed to the model to improve whole-repo work. ([aider.chat](https://aider.chat/docs/repomap.html?utm_source=openai))  
OpenAI’s retrieval guidance similarly points toward explicit retrieval/file-search infrastructure as a first-class system component. ([platform.openai.com](https://platform.openai.com/docs/guides/retrieval?utm_source=openai))

### 5. Evaluation loops are becoming productized
Continue’s local checks and `/check` workflow show that teams are increasingly formalizing AI evaluation/guardrails as reusable artifacts run locally and iterated over. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/checks/running-locally?utm_source=openai))  
That validates your competency-question direction.

---

## What wheels you should probably **not** reinvent

### 1. Workspace runtime rule loading
Do **not** invent your own parallel rule-discovery mechanism if Continue already reads `.continue/rules`.  
Use that as the first-class deployment target for Continue. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/customize/rules?utm_source=openai))

### 2. Agent/check execution framework
Continue already has a checks model and local `/check` loop. If your competency questions overlap with “agent checks,” you should at least evaluate whether `.continue/checks/` can host some of them instead of creating a wholly separate runtime harness. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/checks/best-practices?utm_source=openai))

### 3. Repo-map / whole-repo context compression
Aider already has a mature repo map concept. ([aider.chat](https://aider.chat/docs/repomap.html?utm_source=openai))  
You probably should not build a brand-new competing repo summarizer from scratch unless your package has a very specific R/data-science angle that existing tools do not serve.

### 4. Edit-format / diff-application loop
Aider is already specialized here. ([aider.chat](https://aider.chat/docs/more/edit-formats.html?utm_source=openai))  
If `reproducibleai` starts trying to own edit-loop mechanics, that is likely wheel reinvention.

### 5. Generic retrieval infrastructure
OpenAI-style retrieval/vector-store/file-search patterns already exist in multiple ecosystems. ([platform.openai.com](https://platform.openai.com/docs/guides/retrieval?utm_source=openai))  
If you need retrieval later, `reproducibleai` should probably define **what to retrieve and why**, not become a full retrieval platform.

---

## Where `reproducibleai` can still add unique value

This is the critical point: not reinventing wheels does **not** mean the package lacks a role.

### Strong unique value areas

#### A. Methodology layer
Define:
- hybrid frontier/local best practice,
- task-routing guidance,
- governance expectations,
- evaluation expectations.

This is not being fully solved by Continue, Aider, or Copilot docs in your USACE/data-science context. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/customize/rules?utm_source=openai))

#### B. Canonical instruction source + derivation pipeline
This is a real niche:
- one reviewed human-readable source,
- derived local runtime rules,
- governance-preserving transformation.

That is very specific and valuable.

#### C. Repo-governed AI artifact scaffolding
You already have a governance model around:
- `dev/05_plan.md`
- `dev/10_design.md`
- `dev/40_schemas.md`
- decisions/instructions/sessions

That’s differentiated methodology.

#### D. R-package-native evaluation harness
If competency questions can live alongside standard package tests and fixtures, that is a strong architectural advantage for an R package audience.

#### E. Domain-specific context conventions
A general-purpose tool will not know that, for your team, good repo context may include:
- design docs,
- plan files,
- schemas,
- instructions,
- data-science repo conventions.

That’s exactly where `reproducibleai` can specialize.

---

## My strongest gut-check conclusions

### You are **not** reinventing the wrong wheel if `reproducibleai` becomes:
- a **governed methodology layer**,
- a **rule/source derivation layer**,
- a **repo scaffolding layer**,
- an **evaluation layer**,
- and a **task-routing guidance layer**.

### You **are** reinventing wheels if it tries to become:
- a new AI editor client,
- a new general coding agent runtime,
- a new repo map engine,
- a new code edit engine,
- or a new general retrieval platform.

---

## Recommended package architecture changes

Here are the changes I think you likely need.

### Option 1 — Minimal architecture evolution
Keep the package centered on instructions/handlers, but add:
- rule-tier metadata,
- Continue `.continue/rules` scaffolding,
- hybrid/local recipe families,
- competency-question test fixtures.

**Pros**
- Lowest disruption
- Best near-term fit

**Cons**
- May under-model client abstraction long-term

### Option 2 — Add a formal “client adapter” layer
Introduce package concepts like:
- canonical instruction sources
- derived runtime rules
- client adapters
- deployment targets

For example:
- Continue adapter
- future adapter(s) later

**Pros**
- Best long-term architecture
- Clean separation of concerns
- Avoids Continue-specific assumptions leaking everywhere

**Cons**
- Bigger refactor
- More design work now

### Option 3 — Add a formal “evaluation subsystem”
Treat competency questions as first-class package artifacts with:
- fixtures
- prompts
- expected behaviors
- scoring helpers
- maybe client-specific runners later

**Pros**
- Very aligned with your direction
- Distinguishes package from simple scaffolding tools

**Cons**
- Can grow complex if overbuilt early

## My recommendation

I recommend **Option 2 + Option 3**, phased.

### Phase 1
Introduce architecture concepts for:
- canonical source instructions
- local-adapted runtime rules
- client-specific deployment targets

### Phase 2
Implement:
- Continue runtime rule scaffolding
- competency-question evaluation fixtures
- hybrid task-routing documentation

That feels like the right amount of ambition without building a new platform.

---

## Concrete “best-of-breed” tools you’d be smart to leverage

### Continue
Use for:
- workspace-local rules,
- agent/chat/edit integration,
- possibly checks. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/customize/rules?utm_source=openai))

### Aider
Use for:
- repo-map ideas,
- multi-file edit workflows,
- architect/edit separation concepts. ([aider.chat](https://aider.chat/docs/repomap.html?utm_source=openai))

### Existing linters/tests
Per Continue’s best-practice guidance, if something is pattern-matchable, it belongs in linting/tests rather than prompt rules. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/checks/best-practices?utm_source=openai))  
That means `reproducibleai` should explicitly route concerns into:
- runtime rules,
- linting,
- tests,
- checks.

### MCP / external context bridges
GitHub docs point to MCP as part of extending Copilot. ([docs.github.com](https://docs.github.com/en/enterprise-cloud%40latest/copilot/using-github-copilot/coding-agent/best-practices-for-using-copilot-to-work-on-tasks?utm_source=openai))  
Even if you do not implement MCP support now, your architecture should avoid blocking future external-context/tool integrations.

---

## Suggested package architecture stance after this gut-check

I would revise the package direction to something like:

- `reproducibleai` is **not** an AI runtime
- `reproducibleai` is **not** an editor client
- `reproducibleai` is **not** a general retrieval engine
- `reproducibleai` **is** a methodology, governance, derivation, deployment, and evaluation layer for governed AI-assisted development workflows

That is a strong and sane position.

---

## Governance implication

This research is substantive enough that it likely warrants an update to the working architecture artifact:
- `dev/15_local_ai_architecture.md`

Specifically, I think the document should gain:
1. a short **industry alignment / non-goals** section,
2. a sharper statement of **what `reproducibleai` will not build**,
3. a section on **external tools to leverage rather than replace**,
4. package architecture implications around **client adapters** and **evaluation**.

---

## Best next options

### Option A — Update `dev/15_local_ai_architecture.md` with a gut-check section and non-goals
**Pros**
- Best immediate move
- Captures this sanity-check durably
- Prevents future wheel reinvention

### Option B — Skip doc updates and jump straight to `dev/05_plan.md`
**Pros**
- Faster toward execution

**Cons**
- Loses an important architecture boundary clarification

### Option C — Inspect the latest commit you referenced before drafting the update
**Pros**

**Cons**
- Small delay

## My recommendation

I recommend **Option C first**, then **Option A**.

Because you explicitly said “See these changes in commit `37498286d87299f928131265ddb373ef8375625f`,” I should inspect that commit before proposing the doc update.

If you want, I’ll do that next and then give you:

## @mpdougherty

Agreed. Proceed. Clarifications: 1. I am just learning about the role of MCPs. Wow! They seem like exactly the right fit for the key problem I'm struggling with: I'm mostly concerned about improving development infrastructure in a multi-repo, r package API ecosystem. Since I'm just learning about MCPs, shouldn't we first explore the functional role MCPs play in this architecture we are crafting for `reproducibleai`? They seem like a key piece that I'd be remiss in not including in the design.

## Copilot

Yes — MCPs look highly relevant to your actual problem, and I agree they should be explicitly included in the architecture before you go much further.

**Direct answer:** for `reproducibleai`, MCP should probably be treated as a **key integration boundary**, not as a side note. Based on the official MCP architecture, MCP is a client-server protocol where an AI host can connect to one or more MCP servers that expose **tools**, **resources**, and **prompts**; the host aggregates these into a tool registry/context surface the model can use. ([modelcontextprotocol.io](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/docs/learn/architecture?utm_source=openai)) That maps very naturally onto your multi-repo, R-package, governed-artifact ecosystem.

I also reviewed commit `37498286d87299f928131265ddb373ef8375625f`. The revised `dev/15_local_ai_architecture.md` is much closer to the strong, opinionated architecture proposal you wanted. It now has a clearer package position, stronger architecture conclusions, and stronger deployment/evaluation framing. The remaining gap is that it still treats external integrations mostly implicitly. MCP is probably the missing conceptual layer.

## Why MCP matters here

Your core struggle is not just “local vs hosted.” It is:

- multi-repo development infrastructure,
- repo-governed design artifacts,
- R package APIs across repos,
- need for better context than ad hoc prompt stuffing,
- and need for reusable integration patterns across AI clients.

That is exactly where MCP becomes interesting.

Official MCP docs describe three especially relevant primitives:

- **Tools**: callable actions exposed by servers.
- **Resources**: data/content exposed for model context.
- **Prompts**: reusable interaction templates/workflows. ([modelcontextprotocol.io](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/docs/learn/architecture?utm_source=openai))

For `reproducibleai`, those map almost directly to your design space.

## Best interpretation for `reproducibleai`

### Option 1 — Treat MCP as an external client concern only
**What it is**
- `reproducibleai` ignores MCP internally.
- Users separately wire MCP servers into clients like Continue or Copilot.

**Pros**
- Lowest package complexity
- Avoids premature integration design

**Cons**
- Misses a major architecture opportunity
- Leaves multi-repo/context concerns under-modeled

### Option 2 — Treat MCP as a supported integration target, but not a core package abstraction
**What it is**
- `reproducibleai` documents MCP as one way to expose governed context/tools.
- Package still centers on instructions/rules, with optional MCP alignment later.

**Pros**
- Moderate complexity
- Safe incremental path

**Cons**
- May underplay how central MCP is for your actual problem

### Option 3 — Treat MCP as a core architecture boundary for external context, tools, and governed prompts
**What it is**
- `reproducibleai` remains a methodology/governance/deployment package,
- but explicitly designs for MCP-compatible exposure of:
  - governed resources,
  - repo-aware tools,
  - reusable prompts/workflows.

**Pros**
- Best fit for multi-repo API ecosystem goals
- Strongest future-proofing
- Aligns package with an emerging standard rather than bespoke integrations

**Cons**
- Requires careful scope control
- Increases architecture complexity

## My recommendation

I recommend **Option 3**, with an important boundary:

`reproducibleai` should **design for MCP**, but should **not become a generic MCP platform**.

That means:

- yes to MCP-aware package architecture,
- no to trying to rebuild the whole MCP ecosystem.

## The key architectural role MCP can play

The most useful way to think about MCP here is:

### MCP is not “the model”
It is not a replacement for:
- local models,
- hosted models,
- Continue,
- Copilot.

### MCP is the interoperability layer
It is a way to expose governed, structured capabilities to AI clients in a standard form:
- resources,
- tools,
- prompts. ([modelcontextprotocol.io](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/docs/learn/architecture?utm_source=openai))

That means MCP can help solve your “context aware multi-repo ecosystem” problem much better than stuffing more text into prompts.

## Strong architecture fit with your repo

Here is how MCP likely maps to `reproducibleai`.

### 1. Resources
These could expose governed repo artifacts like:
- `dev/10_design.md`
- `dev/05_plan.md`
- `dev/40_schemas.md`
- instruction modules
- decision records
- package metadata
- maybe package/API maps across repos

Official MCP docs describe resources as a core primitive for exposing data/content for model use. ([modelcontextprotocol.wiki](https://modelcontextprotocol.wiki/en/docs/concepts/resources?utm_source=openai))

This is probably the **single most relevant MCP feature** for your current needs.

### 2. Tools
These could expose actions like:
- discover governed artifacts in a repo
- resolve instruction recipes
- summarize package API surfaces
- retrieve schema definitions
- list related repos/packages
- generate local-adapted runtime rules from canonical instructions
- validate rule/instruction consistency

Official MCP architecture explicitly includes tools for AI actions. ([modelcontextprotocol.io](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/docs/learn/architecture?utm_source=openai))

This is probably your **second most relevant MCP feature**.

### 3. Prompts
These could expose reusable workflows like:
- “analyze repository design”
- “draft governed documentation update”
- “compare frontier vs local task fit”
- “prepare local rule variant from canonical instruction source”

Prompts are also a first-class MCP concept. ([modelcontextprotocol.io](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/docs/learn/architecture?utm_source=openai))

This aligns extremely well with your instruction-first philosophy.

## Why this is better than only rules

Rules are still important, but rules alone mainly shape behavior.  
MCP can expose:
- structured context,
- callable capabilities,
- reusable workflows.

That means MCP can help `reproducibleai` move from:
- “instruction installation package”

toward:
- “governed AI workflow architecture package”

without requiring it to build a custom AI client.

## Important caution: don’t overbuild

This is where the sanity-check matters.

You should probably **not** make `reproducibleai`:
- a general-purpose MCP server framework,
- a full registry of arbitrary tools,
- a generic remote execution platform,
- or a full replacement for existing MCP infrastructure.

Instead, the package should likely specialize in:
- **what** governed resources/tools/prompts matter for your methodology,
- **how** to scaffold/expose them,
- **how** to evaluate them,
- **how** to align them with repo governance.

## Security / governance caution

Because MCP exposes tools/resources to AI clients, it also expands the trust boundary. The official docs frame MCP as a client-server architecture with external servers connected to AI applications. ([modelcontextprotocol.io](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/docs/learn/architecture?utm_source=openai)) Recent reporting also highlights serious security concerns in vulnerable MCP implementations, especially around remote code execution and tool trust. ([tomshardware.com](https://www.tomshardware.com/tech-industry/artificial-intelligence/anthropics-model-context-protocol-has-critical-security-flaw-exposed?utm_source=openai))

So for `reproducibleai`, the architecture should probably assume:

- local-first MCP server patterns where possible,
- explicit trust boundaries,
- narrow server scope,
- read-only resource exposure by default,
- carefully limited tool actions,
- governance-aware documentation of security assumptions.

That is especially important for government-adjacent or managed environments.

## What this means for your package architecture

I think the earlier package direction needs a new layer or section.

### Current architecture layers
You now have:
- model
- context
- rule
- workflow
- evaluation

### Suggested addition
Add an **integration layer** or **capability exposure layer**:

#### 6. Integration layer
This should define how governed repo capabilities are exposed to AI clients through standard interfaces, especially MCP.

It would cover:
- resources,
- tools,
- prompts,
- client adapters,
- security/trust boundaries.

## Recommended package stance after this insight

I would revise the package stance to something like:

- `reproducibleai` is not an AI runtime
- `reproducibleai` is not an editor client
- `reproducibleai` is not a generic MCP platform
- `reproducibleai` **is** a governance, methodology, derivation, deployment, evaluation, and integration package for AI-assisted development workflows
- MCP is a preferred integration standard for exposing governed repo-aware capabilities to compatible AI clients

That feels strong, modern, and not overbuilt.

## Best tools/standards you’d be foolish not to leverage

Given your goals, here are the things I now think you’d be remiss not to consider explicitly:

### 1. Continue workspace rules
For client-side repo runtime behavior. ([modelcontextprotocol.io](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/docs/learn/architecture?utm_source=openai))

### 2. MCP
For structured cross-repo tools/resources/prompts exposure. ([modelcontextprotocol.io](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/docs/learn/architecture?utm_source=openai))

### 3. Existing linters/tests/checks
For machine-checkable concerns instead of prompt rules where possible.

### 4. Possibly Aider-style repo-map concepts
Not necessarily Aider itself as a dependency, but the idea that repo-level structural summaries are useful and should not be reinvented blindly.

## What package changes this likely implies

### Option 1 — Just document MCP in the design doc
**Pros**
- Fast
- Low risk

**Cons**
- Underpowered if MCP is truly central

### Option 2 — Add an MCP-aware architecture section and future package responsibility, but defer implementation
**Pros**
- Best current step
- Keeps architecture honest
- Avoids premature code design

**Cons**
- Still conceptual for now

### Option 3 — Immediately redesign the package around MCP abstractions
**Pros**
- Ambitious
- Future-facing

**Cons**
- Too early
- High risk of overdesign

## My recommendation

I recommend **Option 2**.

That means the next artifact update should:

1. add an **industry gut-check / external tools / non-goals** section,
2. add an **MCP section** explaining its functional role,
3. add an **integration layer** to the architecture,
4. clarify that `reproducibleai` should leverage MCP rather than reinvent generic tool/resource plumbing.

## Governance implication

Yes — this is substantive enough to update `dev/15_local_ai_architecture.md` before moving to plan/ADR updates.

## Recommended next step

Here are the best next moves:

### Option A — Update `dev/15_local_ai_architecture.md` with:
- industry gut-check,
- non-goals,
- MCP role,
- integration layer,
- leveraged external tooling

### Option B — First inspect whether the repo already has any MCP-related artifacts or naming conventions
**Pros**
- Maximum inspect-first rigor

**Cons**
- Likely unnecessary unless you’ve already started MCP work in-repo

## My recommendation

Choose **Option A** now.

If you want, I can draft a paste-ready update for `dev/15_local_ai_architecture.md` that adds:

- `## Industry gut-check`
- `## Non-goals`

That would be the best next refinement.

## Quick assessment of your MCP instinct

**Direct answer:** you are probably *not* overreacting. MCP appears to fit your problem unusually well.

- It is also **how AI clients reach governed repo artifacts, multi-repo package relationships, reusable workflows, and controlled actions**.
- MCP is specifically designed around **tools**, **resources**, and **prompts** exposed by servers to AI hosts. ([modelcontextprotocol.io](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/docs/learn/architecture?utm_source=openai))

That means MCP is a much better conceptual match for your design than “add more rules and hope the model infers everything.”

## Best ways to incorporate MCP right now

### Option 1 — Mention MCP only as a future integration possibility
**What it is**
- Add a short note to the design doc.
- Defer real architectural treatment.

**Pros**
- Low commitment
- Low complexity

**Cons**
- Understates its likely importance
- Misses the chance to shape the package correctly now

### Option 2 — Add MCP as a formal integration layer in the architecture, but defer implementation decisions
**What it is**
- Update `dev/15_local_ai_architecture.md` to say MCP is a preferred integration boundary.
- Add local deployment feasibility as a research question.

**Pros**
- Best current move
- Captures the design shift without overcommitting
- Preserves your thought process in git history

**Cons**
- Still leaves technical feasibility open

### Option 3 — Immediately redesign package abstractions around MCP and start implementation planning
**What it is**
- Begin treating `reproducibleai` as partly an MCP-facing package architecture now.

**Pros**
- Most future-facing
- May avoid later refactors

**Cons**
- Too early before deployment feasibility research
- Risks overdesign

## My recommendation

I recommend **Option 2**.

That aligns with your desire for an incremental git-history-friendly update, while acknowledging your new conclusion that MCP may be architecturally central.

## Why MCP should probably be in the architecture

Continue supports MCP servers and describes them as a way to integrate external tools, systems, and databases; in Continue, MCP is configured alongside models, rules, and prompts as part of the overall config model. Continue also says MCP is currently available in **agent mode**. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/customize/mcp-tools?utm_source=openai))

That is highly relevant because your package architecture is already converging on:
- models,
- rules,
- prompts/workflows,
- and now governed context/actions.

MCP cleanly provides a standard place for the last category.

## Functional role MCP can play in `reproducibleai`

I would frame MCP in your architecture like this:

### 1. MCP as a resource exposure layer
MCP resources are a natural fit for governed artifacts like:
- `dev/10_design.md`
- `dev/05_plan.md`
- `dev/40_schemas.md`
- ADRs
- instruction modules
- package metadata
- perhaps cross-repo package/API maps. ([modelcontextprotocol.io](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/docs/learn/server-concepts?utm_source=openai))

This is likely your **highest-value MCP use case**.

### 2. MCP as a tool exposure layer
MCP tools could expose actions such as:
- inspect governed repo artifacts
- resolve instruction recipes
- summarize package API relationships across repos
- retrieve structured schema information
- derive local runtime rules from canonical source instructions
- validate instruction/rule consistency

MCP tools are explicitly model-callable actions in the protocol. ([modelcontextprotocol.io](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2024-11-05/server/tools?utm_source=openai))

### 3. MCP as a workflow/prompt exposure layer
MCP prompts could package workflows like:
- analyze repository design
- draft governed design updates
- compare hosted vs local task fit
- prepare a Continue runtime rule set from canonical instructions

That is very aligned with your instruction-first philosophy. ([modelcontextprotocol.io](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/docs/learn/server-concepts?utm_source=openai))

## Why this matters especially for multi-repo R package ecosystems

Your concern is not a single repo in isolation. It is:
- multiple repos,
- shared package APIs,
- governance artifacts,
- design continuity,
- and likely repeated patterns across team repositories.

MCP gives you a way to expose these as **structured capabilities** instead of relying on:
- giant pasted prompts,
- ad hoc file selection,
- or fragile “please remember these conventions” workflows. ([modelcontextprotocol.io](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/docs/learn/architecture?utm_source=openai))

## What not to do

Even if MCP is central, `reproducibleai` still should **not** try to become:
- a generic MCP platform,
- a generic AI client,
- a generic retrieval engine,
- or a custom agent runtime.

MCP’s own docs emphasize that the protocol focuses on **context exchange**, not how applications use LLMs internally. ([modelcontextprotocol.io](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/docs/learn/architecture?utm_source=openai))

So your package should likely define:
- what governed resources/tools/prompts should exist,
- how to scaffold or derive them,
- how to deploy them safely,
- how to evaluate them,

rather than reimplementing the whole surrounding ecosystem.

## Your deployment-feasibility instinct is also correct

You said you would first need to research local deployment feasibility using:
- Podman containers,
- locally running Node.js apps,
- or Python.

That is exactly the right next technical question.

Why:
- MCP commonly supports **local servers** using STDIO transport. The official docs explicitly discuss connecting local MCP servers and show local execution patterns such as running a server with `npx`. ([modelcontextprotocol.io](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/docs/develop/connect-local-servers?utm_source=openai))
- MCP also has language SDKs and implementation guidance for different languages. ([modelcontextprotocol.io](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/docs/learn/architecture?utm_source=openai))

So the architecture should assume MCP is plausible, but the package should not yet hard-code:
- Node-only,
- Python-only,
- or container-only assumptions.

## Best-practice gut-check

You asked whether this means you’d be stupid not to take advantage of MCP.

My answer: **for your stated problem, yes, you should explicitly account for MCP now.**

Not because MCP solves everything, but because:
- it directly addresses the “connect governed external context/tools/workflows to the AI client” problem,
- Continue already supports MCP,
- and your repo-governance/multi-repo/API concerns are exactly the kind of structured external context problem MCP was meant to address. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/customize/mcp-tools?utm_source=openai))

## Recommended architecture adjustment

I think `dev/15_local_ai_architecture.md` should gain these additions:

### New section: `## Industry gut-check`
Purpose:
- explain that the package should leverage existing standards and tools rather than recreate them.

### New section: `## Non-goals`
State that `reproducibleai` is not:
- an AI runtime,
- an editor client,
- a generic MCP platform,
- or a generic retrieval engine.

### New section: `## MCP in the proposed architecture`
State that MCP is:
- a preferred integration standard for exposing governed resources, tools, and prompts to compatible clients. ([modelcontextprotocol.io](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/docs/learn/architecture?utm_source=openai))

### Add a sixth architecture layer
Add:
- **Integration layer**
  - client adapters,
  - MCP exposure,
  - trust boundaries,
  - local deployment patterns.

### Add implementation questions
Specifically:
- local MCP deployment feasibility under approved platforms,
- Node.js vs Python vs containerized server approaches,
- trust/security boundaries,
- Continue agent-mode implications. Continue’s docs note MCP use in agent mode. ([docs.continue.dev](https://docs.continue.dev/customize/deep-dives/mcp?utm_source=openai))

## Recommended next-step options

### Option A — Incrementally update `dev/15_local_ai_architecture.md` now
**Pros**
- Matches your preferred git-history approach
- Captures the MCP design pivot immediately
- Best governance fit

**Cons**
- Still leaves feasibility research for later

### Option B — Pause drafting and first research MCP deployment feasibility in more detail
**Pros**
- Better technical grounding

**Cons**
- Delays recording an important architecture insight

## My recommendation

I recommend **Option A now**, then a focused MCP feasibility research pass next.

That lets the repo history show:
1. local/hybrid architecture recognized,
2. MCP identified as a likely central integration mechanism,
3. deployment feasibility elevated as a next design question.

    ## Industry gut-check
    
    The current architecture direction is aligned with emerging industry practice in several important ways.
    
    First, modern AI development workflows increasingly behave as multi-layer systems rather than as single-model experiences. In practice, workflow quality depends not only on model quality, but also on retrieval, tool access, context selection, workflow shaping, evaluation, and client integration.
    
    Second, workspace-local runtime rules are now a standard pattern in at least some major AI coding clients. This supports the package direction of separating canonical human-readable instruction sources from client-facing runtime rule artifacts.
    
    Third, explicit integration standards such as MCP increasingly provide a better solution to structured context and tool access than trying to encode all project knowledge directly into prompt text.
    
    Fourth, evaluation is becoming a first-class concern. Teams increasingly need repeatable workflow checks rather than relying only on anecdotal impressions of whether one AI setup “feels better” than another.
    
    These signals support the current direction of `reproducibleai` as a methodology, governance, derivation, deployment, and evaluation layer rather than as a new AI runtime.
    
    This also clarifies an important boundary:
    
    `reproducibleai` should adopt and integrate best-of-breed external tooling where appropriate instead of attempting to replace mature clients, tool protocols, or context delivery mechanisms.
    
    ## Non-goals
    
    To keep the package architecture focused, `reproducibleai` should not attempt to become:
    
    - a general-purpose AI runtime
    - a code editor or IDE client
    - a generic model serving framework
    - a generic retrieval platform
    - a generic agent runtime
    - a generic MCP platform
    
    Instead, the package should focus on:
    
    - governed instruction architecture
    - hybrid workflow methodology
    - client-facing runtime rule derivation and deployment
    - repository-governed AI context conventions
    - evaluation and competency-question design
    - integration patterns for external tools and protocols
    
    ## MCP in the proposed architecture
    
    MCP should be treated as a preferred integration standard in the package architecture.
    
    This is important because the design problem faced by `reproducibleai` is not limited to prompt wording or model selection. The package also needs a way to expose governed repository artifacts, reusable workflows, and structured actions to compatible AI clients in a standard, inspectable form.
    
    In this architecture, MCP is not the model, and it is not the client.
    
    It is the integration boundary through which AI clients can access governed capabilities.
    
    For `reproducibleai`, the most relevant MCP roles are:
    
    - **resources**
      - governed repository artifacts and structured context sources
    - **tools**
      - callable actions related to repository inspection, instruction handling, validation, or derivation workflows
    - **prompts**
      - reusable workflow entrypoints and task templates aligned with package methodology
    
    This makes MCP a strong fit for:
    
    - multi-repository R package ecosystems
    - governed design and schema artifacts
    - cross-repo API understanding
    - reusable workflow patterns
    - structured local context delivery
    
    The package should therefore be designed so that its methodology, artifact structure, and future deployment helpers can support MCP-compatible exposure of governed capabilities.
    
    This does not mean that `reproducibleai` should become a generic MCP platform.
    
    It means the package should explicitly design for MCP-aware integration and should avoid architecture choices that would make MCP support difficult later.
    
    ## External tools to leverage rather than replace
    
    The package should explicitly leverage existing best-of-breed tools where they fit the architecture.
    
    Initial examples include:
    
    - **Continue**
      - as a client integration target for workspace-local rules, local and hybrid model use, and MCP-aware workflows
    - **MCP**
      - as an integration standard for exposing governed resources, tools, and prompts
    - **existing package tests and linters**
      - for machine-checkable concerns that should not be enforced only through prompt instructions
    - **existing local model runtimes**
      - such as Ollama, rather than custom model-serving logic inside the package
    
    This architecture should prefer integration and specialization over reinvention.

Part 2 of 3 — replace `## Proposed architecture layers` with the following stronger version

    ## Proposed architecture layers
    
    `reproducibleai` should standardize local and hybrid AI methodology across six architecture layers.
    
    ### 1. Model layer
    The architecture should distinguish between different model roles rather than assuming one model should do everything.
    
    The package methodology should assume that tasks such as:
    - explanation
    - planning
    - editing
    - autocomplete
    - summarization
    - repository analysis
    
    may be better served by different model roles, runtime settings, or clients.
    
    The package does not need to hard-code one universal model strategy, but it should explicitly teach model-role separation as a best practice.
    
    ### 2. Context layer
    The architecture should standardize deliberate context selection.
    
    The package should teach that better context is often more important than more context, especially for local models.
    
    Recommended context patterns should include:
    - current file only
    - selected text plus current file
    - current file plus one or two directly related files
    - working file plus governed repository artifact such as:
      - `dev/10_design.md`
      - `dev/05_plan.md`
      - `dev/40_schemas.md`
      - relevant instruction or rule files
    
    The package should discourage indiscriminate broad workspace injection as a default workflow.
    
    ### 3. Rule layer
    The architecture should standardize rule tiers.
    
    The package should distinguish between:
    - **core rules**
      - short, always-on, cross-cutting constraints
    - **task overlays**
      - domain- or workflow-specific runtime rules used only when relevant
    - **source instructions**
      - canonical human-readable reviewed instructions that serve as the source of truth
    - **local-adapted runtime rules**
      - compressed or operationalized variants derived from canonical source instructions for use in local client rule systems
    
    This distinction should become a formal part of package methodology rather than an ad hoc practice.
    
    ### 4. Workflow layer
    The architecture should standardize workflow modes instead of assuming one generic chat protocol is sufficient.
    
    Candidate workflow modes should include:
    - explain current file
    - compare options before editing
    - review before drafting
    - draft replacement text
    - analyze repository design
    - prepare governed documentation updates
    - evaluate task fit for hosted vs local execution
    
    The package should help teams define and reuse these modes consistently.
    
    ### 5. Evaluation layer
    The architecture should make evaluation a first-class concern.
    
    AI workflow quality should be evaluated with repeatable competency questions rather than anecdotal impressions alone.
    
    Evaluation should focus on questions such as:
    - does the workflow use the intended repository context correctly?
    - does it follow the applicable rules reliably?
    - does it produce useful and reviewable outputs?
    - does it choose an appropriate workflow mode for the task?
    - where does local outperform hosted, and where does it not?
    
    This evaluation model should become part of the package’s methodology and testing approach.
    
    ### 6. Integration layer
    The architecture should explicitly define how governed repository capabilities are exposed to AI clients and external workflow components.
    
    This layer should include:
    - client-specific runtime rule deployment
    - MCP-aware exposure of resources, tools, and prompts
    - integration boundaries between package-governed artifacts and external AI systems
    - trust and deployment assumptions for local and hybrid integrations
    
    The integration layer is where `reproducibleai` should connect its governed internal artifacts to external AI tooling without becoming a generic AI platform itself.

Part 3 of 3 — replace `## Proposed package direction` and `## Remaining implementation questions` with the following

    ## Proposed package direction
    
    Based on the current analysis, `reproducibleai` should move toward the following package direction:
    
    - support both frontier and local workflows as first-class methodology targets
    - document hybrid best-bet task routing rather than requiring one universal runtime
    - preserve canonical human-readable instruction sources
    - support derived local-runtime rule variants
    - scaffold workspace-local runtime rules beginning with Continue
    - formalize rule tiers as part of package methodology
    - formalize workflow modes as part of package methodology
    - formalize competency-question-based evaluation as part of package methodology
    - support MCP-aware integration as part of package methodology
    - expose governed capabilities through external-tool-friendly architecture rather than prompt text alone
    
    ## Remaining implementation questions
    
    The major architecture direction is now clear enough to state strongly.
    
    The remaining open questions are primarily implementation and packaging questions rather than core direction questions.
    
    ### 1. Rule derivation mechanics
    Open implementation questions:
    - which parts of local-rule derivation can be automated safely
    - which parts require mandatory human editing or approval
    - how derivation lineage should be recorded
    
    ### 2. Client support sequence
    Open implementation questions:
    - what the first Continue-specific scaffolding should include
    - how client-specific runtime artifacts should be abstracted so later clients can be supported without redesign
    
    ### 3. MCP deployment pattern
    Open implementation questions:
    - which governed resources should be exposed first through MCP
    - which tools should be exposed first through MCP
    - which prompts or workflow templates belong in MCP versus static instruction modules
    - whether MCP helpers should be generated by the package, documented by the package, or both
    
    ### 4. Evaluation harness design
    Open implementation questions:
    - how competency questions should be represented in package tests
    - what fixtures and scoring conventions should be used
    - how local-context-aware behavior should be tested consistently
    - how MCP-enabled workflows should be evaluated alongside non-MCP workflows
    
    ### 5. Recipe and deployment structure
    Open implementation questions:
    - whether frontier-oriented and local-oriented workflows should be represented as separate recipe families
    - how hybrid workflow guidance should be exposed to users
    - how much deployment logic belongs in handlers versus supporting helpers
    - whether MCP-aware capabilities should be treated as a separate config-aware package capability

# My recommended architecture direction

I recommend that the package sketch center on **five MCP-aware package concepts**.

## 1. Canonical source instructions
These stay as your reviewed human-readable source of truth.

Examples:
- `inst/instructions/*.md`

Role:
- reviewed by humans,
- versioned in Git,
- source for runtime derivations,
- source for prompts/workflow templates where applicable.

## 2. Runtime rule artifacts
These are client-facing, derived or adapted artifacts.

Examples:
- Continue workspace rules
- maybe future client-specific rule bundles

Role:
- optimized for runtime behavior,
- scoped to client needs,
- derived from canonical instructions where possible.

## 3. Governed repository resources
These are repo artifacts that should be made available as structured AI context.

Examples:
- `dev/10_design.md`
- `dev/05_plan.md`
- `dev/40_schemas.md`
- ADRs
- package metadata
- maybe package/API summaries

MCP fit:
- prime candidates for **resources**

## 4. Governed tools
These are explicit, bounded actions relevant to your methodology.

Examples:
- resolve instruction recipe
- list governed artifacts
- summarize package API map
- validate instruction/runtime-rule consistency
- generate derived Continue rule files
- compare repo artifacts for drift

MCP fit:
- prime candidates for **tools**

## 5. Workflow prompts/templates
These are reusable task entrypoints aligned with your methodology.

Examples:
- analyze repo design
- draft governed design update
- prepare local rule variant
- evaluate local vs hosted fit for a task

MCP fit:
- prime candidates for **prompts**

# Strong package boundary recommendation

Here is the most important boundary I’d recommend codifying:

## `reproducibleai` should define and scaffold governed capabilities,
but should not own general MCP server infrastructure.

That means:

### Package should likely do
- define what resources matter
- define what tools matter
- define what prompts matter
- scaffold files/config/metadata for supported clients
- maybe generate helper artifacts for local MCP deployment
- document deployment patterns
- test/validate governed outputs

### Package should likely not do
- become a general MCP runtime host
- become a universal server framework
- manage arbitrary remote integrations
- replace existing MCP SDK/server tooling

This is the cleanest “don’t reinvent the wheel” stance.

---

# Best package architecture options

## Option A — Documentation-first MCP architecture
**What it is**
- `reproducibleai` defines the concepts and deployment patterns first.
- Minimal code changes initially.

**Pros**
- Lowest risk
- Good for governance-first design

**Cons**
- Slower path to hands-on testing

## Option B — Scaffolding-first MCP architecture
**What it is**
- Package starts generating MCP-related scaffolds early:
  - local config examples,
  - server templates,
  - resource manifests,
  - prompt templates.

**Pros**
- More concrete
- Faster learning loop

**Cons**
- Can overcommit to an immature structure

## Option C — Thin abstraction + scaffolding
**What it is**
- Define minimal package abstractions now,
- generate only the first small supported target,
- likely Continue + a local MCP deployment pattern.

**Pros**
- Best balance
- Enough structure without overbuilding

**Cons**
- Requires some up-front abstraction design

## My recommendation

I recommend **Option C**.

That means the next architecture sketch should propose:
- minimal abstractions,
- narrow first implementation scope,
- Continue as first runtime rule target,
- MCP as first integration-standard target,
- local deployment assumptions only.
